#6896. Zendaya Channels Dune in a Glittering Sculptural Gown at the 2026 Governors Awards

Zendaya is back on the awards show circuit in a glittering vintage gown meant to kick off her Dune: Part Three press tour. The actress attended this evening’s Governors Awards in a sculptural look from Giles Deacon’s fall/winter 2006 collection. She accessorized with a vintage Rolex watch and jewelry from Chopard.

Her stylist, Law Roach, wrote on Instagram that it’s her first Dune press tour look. The dress is even more stunning in motion:

The star posed alone, though her husband, Tom Holland, was at the event, too.

It marks her first awards show appearance since the Emmys in September, where she wore a glitzy Prada gown.

Dune: Part Three is the last of Zendaya’s five projects to come out this year. She first promoted The Drama and Euphoria season 3, which had April release dates, then The Odyssey and Spider-Man: Brand New Day, which debuted in July. In her ELLE Summer Issue cover story, she acknowledged it’s been a jam-packed 2026 with all the press tours.

“It’s going to be a long year, but what’s great is that I’m really proud of everything. I just really love my job,” she said. “But I think that after this, I’m going to have to take a little bit of a break. [Though] as soon as I take a break, I start to go crazy. And I’m like, ‘I need to work.’”

She also recalled filming all the projects and juggling the roles. “I remember being on set for Euphoria; it was a night shoot at a ranch,” she said. “I was so tired, but I was also learning my Chakobsa lines for Dune. And then I started writing out my lines to memorize for that quick turnaround trip I was going to make to Iceland [for The Odyssey]. It’s not like I have a lot of lines in The Odyssey, but I was working with Christopher Nolan! The most embarrassing thing in life would be messing up my lines, which did happen once.”

The Odyssey director Christopher Nolan, meanwhile, had nothing but praise for Zendaya’s performance, telling ELLE, “We would be in the maddest, craziest situations, just all of us fighting the elements, tearing our hair out, all these things going on, and she would sort of parachute in from her other job with this sense of true grace and poise.”

#6895. Study: Sign-In Problems Are Primary Drivers of Help Desk Tickets

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a new study from ControlUp.

Analyzing 1,069,110 help desk tickets, the IT operations company identified password resets, account lockouts, and multifactor authentication (MFA) issues as major drivers of support demand.

At one large enterprise, account-related tickets took a median of 11 minutes to resolve, compared with 50 hours for network problems. Meanwhile, 87% of network and phone tickets were reassigned between teams, compared with 20% of account tickets.

MFA enrollment and re-enrollment problems appeared at 40% of organizations, with one in five linking these issues to employees replacing or upgrading their phones. Virtual desktop problems appeared at 55% of organizations analyzed.

Traditional phone support remained a major channel for IT assistance.

Among organizations that recorded how support requests were submitted, 57% reported that phone calls accounted for approximately two-thirds or more of contacts. At one organization, that figure reached 99%.

Machine-generated tickets from monitoring, security, backup, and patching systems appeared at half of the organizations studied. At 15%, automated tickets represented between 25% and 50% of all requests.

The research covered enterprises across retail, healthcare, financial services, manufacturing, construction, energy, and IT services.

ControlUp said the findings highlight the need to evaluate recurring problems and their operational impact rather than relying solely on ticket volume and resolution speed.

"IT organizations have traditionally measured support performance by how quickly they close tickets, but that doesn't tell the whole story," said Simon Townsend, senior vice president of marketing and office of the CTO at ControlUp.

"A password reset that takes a few minutes and an infrastructure failure that disrupts an entire location shouldn't carry the same operational weight."

For more information, go to the ControlUp site.

Network-provider trust concerns are impacting enterprise AI plans, with AI and data-driven projects the most affected initiatives among respondents reporting disruption, according to a report from telecommunications provider Arelion.

Research released around the recent Black Hat USA 2026 conference demonstrates that artificial intelligence is increasing the speed and scale of cybersecurity activity without replacing the attack methods defenders already face.

Global ransomware activity increased 23% in the third quarter of 2026, with organizations in North America and Europe accounting for the largest share of attacks, according to cybersecurity firm NCC Group.

#6891. Enterprise AI Spending Outpaces Adoption Growth, Report Finds

Enterprise AI spending is growing faster than the number of organizations paying for it, according to a new Menlo Ventures report that points to a shift from buying access for employees to financing work performed by agents.

The venture capital firm's 2026 State of Enterprise AI report estimates deployment spending reached a $176.8 billion annualized run rate by September, up from $33 billion in 2025. The share of enterprises paying for AI rose from 55% to 65%.

A larger bill can reflect broader use inside an organization, but it does not independently establish that the additional work is useful or profitable.

Menlo's methodology describes revenue figures as annualized run rates. The $176.8 billion estimate therefore should not be read as $176.8 billion already spent during the first nine months of 2026.

The report also distinguishes unusually heavy spenders from the typical respondent. Average reported spending reached 4.7 times the prior year's level, while the median reached 2.2 times. That gap suggests a smaller group of large spenders drove the average substantially.

The practical implication is that an industry growth figure may be a poor benchmark for an individual organization's budget. Buyers need to understand their own workload growth and the results it produces.

"Agents are changing the economics of software," Menlo principal Derek Xiao said in the firm's announcement.

Menlo reports that agents account for 80% of tokens on OpenRouter, a service providing access to multiple models. That measures activity on one platform, rather than all enterprise AI use. Token consumption also measures processing volume, rather than completed business outcomes.

An agent that makes repeated attempts can consume more resources without delivering proportionately more value. For procurement teams, the relevant comparison is consequently the cost of completing acceptable work, including the review it requires.

Menlo estimates that Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google capture 69% of enterprise AI deployment spending. That measure is distinct from their shares of enterprise language-model API spending, a narrower category. The figures should not be used interchangeably.

The report draws on a Morning Consult survey of nearly 400 U.S. enterprise decision-makers responsible for more than $4 billion in AI spending. Market totals are modeled estimates informed by surveys, public filings, and third-party reports. Menlo discloses investments in Anthropic and OpenRouter.

A separate announcement from Information Services Group describes buyers putting more emphasis on the economics and reliability of production deployments. ISG says ongoing security and observability work remains necessary as organizations scale.

That provides a useful distinction between market demand and business value. Suppliers can generate growing revenue while customers still determine which deployments justify the cost.

For enterprise leaders, the new spending estimate strengthens the case for examining usage beyond license counts. It leaves the harder purchasing question with the customer: How much worthwhile work did the increased spending actually buy?

Menlo's State of Enterprise AI report is available here.

#6886. Your Horoscope for the Week of November 15

Last week, Mercury Retrograde and Venus Retrograde finally ended. This week, you get a moment to catch your breath as Scorpio season draws to a close (it'll end next Sunday, November 22). Along with enjoying the remainder of the Scorpio vibes while they last, there are two smaller cosmic events that you might notice this week.

First, Jupiter and Neptune Retrograde form a harmonious biquintile aspect on Sunday. In English, this means that the two planets are connecting in such a way that their energies are working together. You might notice a little extra luck or motivation today. Then, on Tuesday, the First Quarter Moon in Aquarius continues the "get to work" message. A First Quarter Moon marks the halfway point between a New Moon and a Full Moon, and it's a reminder to stay focused on your goals.

Read your Sun/Rising sign horoscope for the week of November 15:

Make something, Aries. Sunday's Jupiter-Neptune aspect ignites your creativity and your motivation. Start working on that novel/album/podcast/whatever you've been thinking about. Then, on Tuesday, the First Quarter Moon encourages you to reach out to your friends for support or advice as you work on this project.

Phone home, Taurus. On Sunday, the Jupiter-Neptune connection could turn your thoughts towards your childhood. Now's a great time to catch up with a family member or old friend. Then, on Tuesday, the First Quarter Moon gets you even more motivated to work hard at the office. This is a crucial time of year for you, and you're determined to make a good impression.

Catch up with your friends, Gemini. You're feeling even more social than usual under Sunday's Jupiter-Neptune aspect. Go out to brunch with your friends or at least talk to your bestie on the phone. On Tuesday, the First Quarter Moon encourages you to explore beyond your bubble by meeting new people, studying a new subject, or traveling somewhere new to you.

Ask for that promotion, Cancer. Sunday's Jupiter-Neptune aspect gives you some extra luck when it comes to money matters and some extra bravery when it comes to your career. (If you don't work weekends, ask your boss to talk on Monday instead.) On Tuesday, the First Quarter Moon reminds you that you're in the middle of a personal transformation. Who is the New You?

Yes, you should get bangs, Leo. Sunday's Jupiter-Neptune connection could make you want to experiment with your appearance, and with Venus Retrograde in the rearview mirror, now's the time. Then, on Tuesday, the First Quarter Moon asks you to check in on your relationship. If you're partnered, is there anything you've been meaning to bring up? If you're single, do you want to change anything about your dating life?

Let it go, Virgo. Sunday's Jupiter-Neptune biquintile makes it easier for you to leave something in the past so you can move forward unencumbered. Something (or someone) in your life is past its expiration date. On Tuesday, the First Quarter Moon helps you change up your daily routine by starting a new good habit or breaking a bad one.

Friends to lovers, Libra? Sunday's Jupiter-Neptune connection blurs the line between romance and friendship, helping you grow closer to your partner or make sparks fly with a friend you've been crushing on. Then, on Tuesday, the First Quarter Moon gives you creative inspiration. Follow it wherever it leads and take notes, because these vibes won't last for long.

Get to work, Scorpio. On Sunday, the Jupiter-Neptune aspect reminds you that the tedious day-to-day tasks can add up into a big career move. Make sure you're keeping your professional goals in mind this week. On Tuesday, the First Quarter Moon prompts you to strengthen your relationship with a family member or longtime friend. Today's a good day to reach out.

Go have fun, Sagittarius. Sunday's Jupiter-Neptune connection asks you to prioritize travel, fun, and creativity—your favorite things. After your busy and enjoyable weekend, Tuesday's First Quarter Moon reminds you that your voice is powerful. As a Sagittarius, you infamously have a tendency to put your foot in your mouth, so think through what you want to say before you say it.

Who feels like family, Capricorn? Sunday's Jupiter-Neptune aspect reminds you that your family—both family of origin and chosen family—can fuel your transformation, whether away from or alongside them. Reflect on the things you've learned from the people closest to you. On Tuesday, the First Quarter Moon draws your attention to your finances and your values. Are your spending habits in line with your ideals? In other words, are you putting your money where your mouth is?

Who's your other half, Aquarius? On Sunday, the Jupiter-Neptune aspect helps you bring up an important subject with whoever that person is—whether a romantic partner, best friend, family member, or creative collaborator. The conversation might be tricky, but it'll be worth it. On Tuesday, the First Quarter Moon is all about you. Go ahead and treat yourself today.

How's your budget, Pisces? Sunday's Jupiter-Neptune connection reminds you that your small savings—and purchases—can add up over time. Revise your budget and see if you need to make any changes. Then, Tuesday's First Quarter Moon helps you let go of something that's been holding you back, like a toxic person, a self-limiting belief, or an idea that's just not working out.

#6885. Warmth and Whimsy: The Blanket Coat Does It All

Is this fashion’s most comforting coat of all time?

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Fashion is all wrapped up in wraps, shawls, and capes. Call them what you will, but at the end of the day, they’re truly elevated blanket coats. For further proof, look at the fall/winter 2026 runways, where Colleen Allen and TWP led the charge with models dripping in comforting supersized scarf shawls turned coats. Elsewhere, at Fforme, models were decked out in huge swaths of furry fabrics that completely enveloped the wearers, as if swaddled. At Khaite, dresses were styled with large cocooning blanket-like throws trailing off the backs. Blanket-like pieces also emerged from stiff leather coats at Altuzarra and cascaded off the shoulders at Proenza Schouler.

Aside from being easy to wear and warm, the blanket coat offers a different kind of feel than your average black puffer might. In a tumultuous world, the blanket coat serves as a literal, wearable wrap of warmth and a representation of physical and metaphorical well-being. Not unlike the puffy statement scarf, a blanket coat is deeply practical but also inherently whimsical in spirit, even if not aesthetically so. It’s almost a little bit like taking your bed out the door with you. You already have the pillow by way of the puffed scarf. Why not bring the blanket too? And while you’re at it, don’t forget grandma’s couch in the form of the trending tapestry bag. It’s all about tactile touches.

Designers are clearly and intentionally leaning into the feeling of comfort: For TWP’s aforementioned fall/winter 2026 options, founder Trish Wescoat Pound referenced hot toddies, jigsaw puzzles, and fireplaces, in addition to other warm and cozy-feeling fabrics like Scottish cashmere in her show notes. In a more simplified vein, for Rachel Scott’s Proenza Schouler debut, where blanket-like shawls cascaded off the shoulders, the show notes described the pieces as clothes that “move easily between contexts. They are for a new New York woman, a global woman, who is aware of what the world asks of her but is unwilling to acquiesce to expectations.” Phoebe Philo’s recent take on the trend comes in the form of a brown and bronze houndstooth wool blanket coat with a hand-frayed hem.

Smaller brands like LilyEve also make a good business out of the blanket coat; the brand, in particular, upcycles hand-sourced vintage Hermès blankets and re-creates them into one-of-a-kind coats. “Seeing those little pieces and trying to think of what other product could come out of that, whether it’s tiny pieces of toweling or silk left from the scarves, that’s always a really fun inspiration,” she told ELLE earlier this year.

Certainly, there’s no arguing that we’re all craving comfort at the moment. Fashion’s obsession with blankets as clothing on the runway just further proves it.

#6884. Phishing Threats Totaled 7.3 Billion in Q3 2026

E-mail phishing threats reached 7.3 billion in Q3 2026, with September activity increasing 41% from August, according to Microsoft Threat Intelligence.

According to a report from Microsoft Threat Intelligence and the Microsoft Defender Security Research Team, phishing detections reached nearly 3 billion in September alone.

The findings also highlighted changes in attacker techniques, including business-service impersonation, credential theft, and social engineering through Microsoft Teams.

The Redmond-based firm recognized approximately 129 million malicious messages spoofing the sender domains of tracked business services during Q3.

DocuSign was the most frequently spoofed brand in September, accounting for 29% of the month's tracked spoofing activity. Adobe, SurveyMonkey, and Barracuda also experienced increases toward the end of the quarter.

The research found that some malicious messages were delivered despite failing e-mail authentication checks and being identified as threats.

In September, tenant- or user-level security overrides applied to 63% of these messages with a recorded security outcome — most involved tenant-level exceptions.

Microsoft warned that overly broad exceptions intended to support legitimate business communications can weaken protection against impersonation attacks.

The company emphasized that these findings do not establish whether recipients opened the messages or whether accounts were compromised.

Microsoft also recorded approximately 141,000 malicious Teams call attempts during Q3.

Attackers increasingly used ordinary personal names instead of explicitly impersonating IT support personnel. Such display names accounted for 46% of an observed sample in July, rising to 88% in September.

Meanwhile, QR code phishing declined 67% compared to Q2, totaling roughly 12.1 million attacks.

CAPTCHA-gated phishing activity increased toward the end of the quarter, reaching 3.1 million detected attacks in September. SVG attachments played a significant role in the increase.

Credential theft remained the primary objective of malicious attachment campaigns, with approximately 94% to 96% of payload-based attacks directing users to phishing sites or fraudulent sign-in pages.

Microsoft recommended reviewing Exchange Online Protection and Defender for Office 365 configurations, enabling Safe Links and Safe Attachments, and strengthening authentication protections.

The company also advised organizations to review security exceptions and verify the identities of external Teams contacts before responding to suspicious requests.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog post.

Network-provider trust concerns are impacting enterprise AI plans, with AI and data-driven projects the most affected initiatives among respondents reporting disruption, according to a report from telecommunications provider Arelion.

Research released around the recent Black Hat USA 2026 conference demonstrates that artificial intelligence is increasing the speed and scale of cybersecurity activity without replacing the attack methods defenders already face.

Microsoft has shut down EvilTokens, a cybercrime service that used AI to help attackers break into e-mail accounts, study their victims, and build convincing financial scams.

Global ransomware activity increased 23% in the third quarter of 2026, with organizations in North America and Europe accounting for the largest share of attacks, according to cybersecurity firm NCC Group.

#6879. Is This a ‘Gut Feeling’ About My Relationship... or Just My Anxiety?

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

We’re often told that we should trust our gut in relationships—that when something is wrong, we’ll get a distinct feeling in our bodies. But what if that feeling isn’t distinct at all? What if these feelings of dread and alarm are constant? What if you can’t really trust your gut?

I’ve had anxiety for as long as I can remember. And when I get into relationships, I feel the symptoms tenfold. I create worst-case scenarios in my head, over-analyze every little reaction, and go into a panic when a partner has the slightest shift in attitude. A lot of this anxiety has to do with my past (surprise, surprise). I was in an emotionally abusive relationship, riddled with infidelity and manipulation, for six years. And although it’s been years since my breakup and I’ve moved on to an entirely new relationship that feels healthy and fulfilling, I don’t think my body and brain have quite gotten the memo.

“A person who has experienced neglect, abandonment, unpredictability or inconsistencies in past relationships may be unknowingly conditioned to scan for threat, even in a healthy, stable relationship,” Jessica N. Gordon, LCSW tells Cosmopolitan.

So if you come equipped with intense relational trauma, how do you know the difference between when something is actually wrong in your relationship—and when it’s just your anxiety acting up again?

When every little thing feels like the end of the world, it becomes hard to discern when there’s a real concern in your relationship. This is typically what Relationship Obsessive Compulsive Disorder (ROCD) looks like.

"Anxiety driven by ROCD tends to feel very intense and urgent,” Cameron Murphey, LMFT says. “It feels like you have to address it right now.” He explains that people with ROCD typically feel like they’re unable to focus on anything else until they've addressed what's making them feel anxious.

But the problem is what’s making them anxious isn’t exactly rooted in something concrete. People with ROCD constantly ruminate on thoughts like “How do I know I’m truly in love?,” “There have been no signs, but I think they’re cheating on me,” or “Is my partner doing enough?” There might not even be a specific action that triggers these thoughts.

“An [ROCD] thought tends to jump from topic to topic... and feels spontaneous rather than a consistent subtle thought,” Gordon says. A true concern in the relationship, on the other hand, is tied to real, observable behavior that shows up in a pattern: “every time we talk about my feelings, he picks up his phone and I feel ignored.”

But what if your gut is just screaming at you that something is wrong?

“I think we sometimes give our gut feelings a lot of authority without stopping to consider what's informing them,” Sonni Williams, LPC says. “Our intuition can be shaped by our experiences, values, and even the things we're afraid of repeating.”

She adds that we sometimes tend to oversimplify intuition as calm and anxiety as panic. The problem with this is that both of these things can feel convincing, and neither feeling alone tells us whether something is true.

Rather than torturing yourself with these thoughts as they come, Williams says it’s more effective and helpful to look at what happens afterward.

“Can you reflect on the concern and decide whether something needs attention?” she says to ask yourself. “Or are you repeatedly analyzing conversations and feelings, trying to find certainty?”

Now this doesn’t mean you need an immediate answer in order to take your feelings seriously. Williams explains that when it comes to concerns involving abuse, coercion, or controlling behaviors, these things deserve attention regardless of whether anxiety or ROCD are present.

It’s absolutely a good thing to reflect on your relationship and assess if it’s working for you. But Williams explains that what’s most important is knowing the difference between evaluating your relationship and feeling like you have to constantly prove that it’s right or perfect.

And you may not be able to trust your gut, specifically, but that doesn’t mean you shouldn’t trust yourself—you just have to learn what that looks like in the context of your anxiety.

"Self-trust is not about believing that you'll always make the right choice,” Murphey says. “It's about trusting that you can handle whatever happens, good or bad.”

He explains that we tend to feel fear in our stomachs, so it’s easy to confuse those very physical feelings with something grander like intuition. And guess what? That’s totally fine—so long as you don’t use those feelings to solely dictate your actions in your relationship. If you get those feelings, think about why or where they’re coming from.

You’re going to have second thoughts about your partner from time-to-time (or if you’re me, every three business days). Seeing a therapist for lingering, uncontrollable thoughts can help you work through whatever anxieties you may have. Otherwise, there are going to be times where you just inevitably question your relationship.

Gordon says, “The goal is to feel less controlled by doubt, not to never have it.”

#6878. Ronny Kobo Wants You to Embrace Holiday Dressing Clichés

The designer discusses the merits of tuxedo dressing and her new Brooks Brothers holiday capsule.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

For years, Ronny Kobo has turned to Brooks Brothers for one piece in particular: a white tuxedo jacket. “If you know a good white tuxedo menswear jacket, the best in the market is available at Brooks Brothers,” the designer tells ELLE. “Every couple of years I refreshed my wardrobe with a white tuxedo men’s size 50. And when I went to the store about a year ago, I started to daydream and I was thinking, wouldn’t it be amazing to collaborate with Brooks Brothers?”

That daydream wasn’t far-fetched; it became the starting point for Kobo’s holiday 2026 capsule with Brooks Brothers. Reworking the brand’s tuxedo and evening wear codes through a feminine lens, the collection gives sharp tailoring, satin lapels, and classic tartan a softer turn in fluid dresses and statement-making separates.

For Kobo, the collaboration was an opportunity to pair Brooks Brothers’ menswear heritage with tried-and-true holiday codes, from green and red tartan to silk bow details, clichés and all.

“Holiday dressing should be about the cliches,” she notes. “What’s fun is showing up to a holiday party in a cliche. That’s what makes your holiday your holiday party. Let’s create something that is so holiday in your closet that you take out every holiday for the rest of your fashion life.”

Despite those seasonal touches, many of the pieces can be worn year-round. Tailored staples, including a series of tuxedo jackets and formal trousers, work well beyond the holidays. Accessories feel just as seasonless, with cummerbunds, leather opera gloves, and a red rosette brooch bringing a touch of occasion to a look in any season.

The designer achieved that year-round appeal through pieces that can be styled more than one way. Kobo’s take on the white tuxedo jacket is elongated and nipped at the waist, doubling as a minidress; it could just as easily be layered with the high-waisted Ruby pant or worn over the sheer lace Beryl top. The collection is made for “going out,” with dressier options including a high-low skirt with a cummerbund waist, a fitted LBD with a red sash, and the Lazuli dress, a body-conscious stretch-satin number with a flouncy peplum.

The collection’s mix-and-match pieces reflect Kobo’s belief that black tie should leave room for individual expression.

“Allowing your personal style to shine through these tuxedo moments is really important,” she says. “Men get to show up in black tie and express their personality in such a quiet, elegant way, because you can’t put too much on a tuxedo jacket. With this collection, we’re just supplying the canvas. I’d love to see how everyone styles it, whether with lace stockings, velvet, or some kind of conversation piece that feels authentic to you.”

“Mark had to readjust some of the choreo.”

“Mark had to readjust some of the choreo for her on next week’s dance,” a source tells The Sun. “They also had to postpone two different rehearsals so she can rest. But she’s pushing through the pain—which is getting worse every week but she’s determined to not let it stop her.”

Here’s Maura arriving to rehearsals on November 15, so clearly she’s still in it to win it.

Viewers saw Maura experience rib pain during rehearsals for Grammy Week, and she told Entertainment Tonight after the show that she’d ended up in the hospital. “It was quite a traumatic week. I’m not gonna lie,” she said. “Like we lost so much rehearsal time. I ended up in the hospital. I couldn’t really breathe. The swelling was so bad. But I didn’t want to give up. I didn’t want to throw the towel in. And I literally was like I just need to fight through this. I’m just glad I’m still here. And I’m feeling so much better now.”

Maura also confirmed that while X-rays showed there’s no crack in her rib, muscle and cartilage damage would “feel the exact same” and that the only treatment is to wait and let it heal.

Here’s to hoping Maura gets a chance to rest up and heal ahead of the DWTS semifinals Tuesday night!

Thalberg Memorial Award. But before the awards were presented, stars galore arrived on the red carpet in their finest finery.

The actress paired the sculptural, hip-forward look with dazzling Chopard jewelry and a slim Rolex timepiece. Elsewhere, ELLE Women in Hollywood honorees Penélope Cruz, Julianne Moore, Florence Pugh, Carrie Coon, and Inde Navarette brought the fashion in Chanel, Bottega Veneta, Valentino, Balenciaga, and Dior, respectively. Primary hues—particularly a stark cherry red color—proved to be a popular choice among stars, most notably Charlize Theron, in Givenchy by Sarah Burton, and Lea Seydoux, who dazzled in a Louis Vuitton train gown. The evening also made room for menswear, with style gurus Pedro Pascal and Alexander Skarsgård rewriting the rules of black-tie dressing in unconventional looks by Chanel and Tom Ford.

The source added that “The time commitment to Wheel of Fortune exceeds his schedule for filming Idol, which is why he is paid more for Wheel. He doesn’t make as much as Pat, but when he renews his contract, he’ll get a significant pay bump.”

And please be advised this is simply one of the many ways Ryan makes money. He also—per Forbes—takes home $1 million for Dick Clark’s New Year’s Rockin’ Eve, a rumored $10 million per season of American Idol, and signed a $25 million podcast deal for On Air With Ryan Seacrest.

Honestly, no idea how this man has time to juggle this many jobs, let alone time to deposit all his various paychecks. But either way, Ryan has amassed a gigantic net worth for himself! According to Celebrity Net Worth, he has $500 million to his name, making him one of THE wealthiest people in the industry.

#6861. From “Magnetic Merlot” to “Soft Licorice,” 10 Dark, Minimalist Manicure Ideas to Try This Winter

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Bolder hues are getting the starring role as winter sets in

While soap manicures, subtle petal nails, and longevity techniques once dominated the summer months, a darker color palette is quickly taking over now that the clocks have gone back and evenings are early. Yet if you're hesitant to venture into "too bold" territory or fear a pop of color on your fingertips, a dark manicure doesn't have to feel overly attention-grabbing.

In fact, as professional nail technician Samantha Rudge shares, dark manicures with a more minimalist finish are proving to be winter's most popular manicure trend. "For this season, I think we'll be seeing darker classic manicures with minimalist touches," she shares, adding that she has already seen an uptick in shade requests for Bio Sculpture's rich, chocolate-toned Decadent Cocoa and the ever-popular Garnet Noir, a moody red-black hue. "Nail designs like chrome, cat eye, micro French tips, and tortoiseshell will also dominate this season," she adds.

"Short nails are also very popular right now, no matter the nail art or shades," she adds. Regardless of shape or color, Rudge stresses the importance of prioritizing nail health at this time of year. To protect nail longevity and prevent chipping, Rudge recommends assessing your natural nail condition, choosing the right base (BIAB or gel), and using a nourishing cuticle oil to repair and strengthen brittle nails.

Ahead, discover 10 new dark manicures for those who love a more minimalist finish, from “Garnet Noir” nails to subtle lines.

Somewhere between a square and an oval, these squoval nails look sophisticated and smart—plus, paired with the deep red “garnet noir” shade, they make a strong case for opting for a shorter length.

The epitome of minimalist nail art, add subtle intrigue to your manicure this winter via a curved line created on the cuticles. Choose complementary shades to recreate it: think cherry red with hot pink; navy blue and khaki green.

Taking over the SS27 runways this season, purple is finally having a moment in the spotlight. When it comes to nail colors, look for richer hues with a jewel-tone finish to keep the manicure elevated and modern. Rudge recommends Bio Sculpture's Erica shade.

All shades of wine are a staple as the festive season approaches, but why not add a chrome effect for extra sparkle?

Coffee tones are becoming one of the most popular color stories in nail salons this winter. These teeny-tiny dots make for a unique switch-up and can be created using a micro dotting tool.

A subtle marble effect elevates an otherwise classic burgundy manicure.

While almond and square are among the most popular of nail shapes, don't underestimate a soft oval. Paired with a classic cabernet tone, this dark manicure strikes the balance of looking impactful yet soft.

If you're taking a break from acrylics or longer lengths, a super-short licorice manicure is the way to do it. Add a glossy top coat and keep the nails short and square.

In the mood to experiment? Simply switch up each nail with chocolate swirls, block colors, or a French tip to recreate this look.

This ultra-glossy midnight blue manicure is all about a high-shine, mirrorlike finish that could be created with a chrome powder or top coat.

#6855. The Complicated Politics of Forgiving a Pop Star

Slayyyter’s Grammy moment is raising bigger questions about what accountability really looks like.

It’s official: Slayyyter has graduated from being a gay guy music video night staple to a full-on Grammy nominee since dropping her first major-label album, WORST GIRL IN AMERICA.

The underdog has seemingly become a Main Pop Girl before our very eyes. She drew a record-breaking 50,000 people at Lollapalooza and her first-ever headlining show at Madison Square Garden sold out in minutes. Her viral hit, “CRANK,” even landed a perfectly placed needle-drop in the equally awards-buzzy film Club Kid. These are only a fraction of the feats that prove her Best New Artist nod has been years in the making and, dare I say, rightfully earned.

But what happens when the pains of her past finally catch up to her ascent? At the height of her breakout year, old social media posts using racial slurs resurfaced and introduced new fans to her regrettable history just as she was building momentum for the Grammys.

Though she had previously taken accountability for her behavior in 2020, she seemingly surrendered to the recent “Slayyyter Is Over Party” conversations fueling Stan Twitter with a post on her private account.

“This newfound attention is cool and all, but it’s unsettling about having people try to find things to ruin me with,” she reportedly wrote. “Why do you want to see me fail so bad?”

Slayyyter isn’t the first pop star to have old social media behavior resurface as her career reaches new heights. Ethel Cain penned a four-page statement acknowledging her racist posts after the controversy surrounding them escalated, including the doxxing of her family and partner. When Camila Cabello’s use of the N-word resurfaced in 2021, she publicly apologized and took part in weekly racial healing sessions. While the circumstances aren’t identical, each situation raises questions about what accountability looks like when a pop star’s past follows them into a new chapter.

“Everyone has to be online to build their brand and promote their work in 2026, but a lot of times, the parasocial relationship that leads to monumental disappointment for some fans when they find out their fave isn't perfect is due to their being online,” comedian and podcast host Akilah Hughes, who called Slayyyter out for her recent response, tells Cosmopolitan.

Hughes only discovered the artist this past year after hearing her songs in several New Music Friday roundups. She admits that she “was definitely paying attention” to each drop and “was ready to be ‘early’ on” her music until those old posts popped up on her social feeds.

“I don’t think Slayyyter is necessarily a victim, though it’s definitely unfortunate that—as she’s put out this great album and is in the midst of a huge ascent—her own behavior is haunting her,” she explains.

For Kiara Smith, a culture writer and podcast host who has followed Slayyyter since 2019, her posts brought a different kind of disappointment. At first, she was skeptical about the scandal’s reemergence.

“It seemed like someone just resurfaced the posts to start drama, rather than actually caring about what she said,” Smith tells Cosmopolitan.

In the heat of the hate train, users allegedly started piling on fabricated screenshots that falsely depicted her as targeting Black recording artist Normani. Smith acknowledges that while she should be held accountable for the real posts, the fake ones serve no additional purpose.

“What she said was terrible and inexcusable, but we have to be accurate when sharing things so as not to cause further harm to Black people for no reason,” Smith shares. “Black trauma is not a hot-button topic to debate. Tweets like that hurt real people and should be treated seriously.”

Hughes echoes a similar sentiment. “For a lot of young people, being the perfection police is a way to gain clout online,” she says. “Having an encyclopedic knowledge of the times everyone has messed up is akin to power.”

She continues, “Offline, in the real world, people are much more willing to give grace and additional chances. Because real life affords nuance, and the internet affords none.

“I think the internet is a very unforgiving place and has no place for nuance,” Smith adds. “If a person fucks up in real life, you can not fuck with them all you want. But in the end, I think everyone should want that person to be better and learn from their mistakes.”

But is that grace extended equally? Can an artist—particularly a female artist—ever really overcome her past mistakes?

Slayyyter did her part years ago, yet she’s forced to revisit her past and educate herself as she reaches new levels of fame. Male counterparts like Chris Brown and Morgan Wallen have historically been awarded second (and third…and fourth) chances for other serious offenses, yet have continued to enjoy commercial success and industry support.

“We know that the internet and the world are way less forgiving to women. If you’re a woman, you have to do 10 times more to come back from a controversy,” Smith says. “Lots of people have had controversies where they say a lot of nothing, but when this first came out years ago, Slayyyter seemed genuinely apologetic and even donated to many Black people and organizations afterwards. Not to say that excuses it, but we have to consider the effort she’s already made.”

Two things can be true: while pop stars should be held accountable for such offenses, they should also be able to reflect and move forward. This isn’t to defend or endorse their actions, but to invite fans and artists to have more productive conversations—especially as this vicious cycle of scarlet letter-stamping continues to be normalized, particularly with female acts.

Accountability doesn’t guarantee forgiveness, nor should it. But that doesn’t mean any possibility of growth should be off the table, and Slayyyter has seemingly come to peace with that.

When describing her personal thesis behind WORST GIRL IN AMERICA to Cosmopolitan back in March, she nearly prophesied what her come-up would inevitably entail.

“You can’t really escape where you’re from,” she said. “You can change and grow into something better, but the things that happen to you when you’re young—you carry them with you for the rest of your life.”

#6842. The 12 Best Round Brushes for a Salon-Level Blowout

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Perfect your next hair look with these expert-loved picks

Perfecting an at-home blowout once required contortionist-level abilities combined with a little patience and a lot of skill, especially when styling the back of your head. But nowadays, thanks to the advancement in hair tools—from high-powered blow-dryers to one of the best round brushes—it's easier than ever to craft sleek styles that rival your local salon without dealing with arm fatigue.

Unlike regular boar-bristle or vented brushes, round brushes feature a cylindrical shape in which hair wraps around for a seamless dry. Though many of these tools are crafted from similar materials, they're also available in a range of sizes to suit your hair texture and unique beauty needs. But regardless of the brush you choose, experts say a polished dry isn't achieved with dripping wet hair. "The key is to get all the moisture out," celebrity hairstylist Priscilla Valles tells Bazaar. "To achieve the smooth look, you need to keep drying until the hair is completely dry."

New York City-based hairstylist Jackson Simmonds recommends having two to three round brushes in your collection—just in case you change your hairstyle. “To keep it simple, think, smaller brushes for shorter lengths, bigger brushes for longer lengths,” he explains.

Here, we've narrowed down some of the best round brushes from Oribe, Sisley Paris, and more, all highly recommended by experts. Whether your hair is short, extra long, curly, or super fine, these top picks can be game-changing to help deliver a bouncy, full-bodied blowout from the comfort of your bathroom.

When we polled hairstylists, several mentioned Olivia Garden's hairbrushes. They win best overall round brush because they're foolproof: They come in a full range of sizes (general rule of thumb: the longer your hair, the larger and longer your barrel should be), and it's hard to get your hair tangled in an Olivia Garden round brush. If you need a place to start, the Ceramic + Ion Thermal Hairbrush is a bestseller. But you can also go through the brand's hairbrush guide to find your perfect fit.

Bristle type: syntheticBarrel material: ceramicSize options: 0.5 inches to 4.25 inches

A boar-bristle round brush is ideal for someone with coarse hair texture, as it provides more stretch and tension than a ceramic or ionic brush. This line is a favorite of stylists like Simmonds, who says, “They have a huge selection of sizes, bristle density, and length, so you have an option for whatever style you're trying to create.” He adds that the cork handle is a plus: “It makes it light and breezy in your hand and never slips away as you're working.”

Bristle type: nylonBarrel material: woodSize options: 35 to 85 millimeters

Oribe crafted a brush that's as efficient as it is pretty. Atop the stunning resin handle is a mix of boar bristles and nylon pins that move through hair without damaging or tugging and help distribute the natural oils in hair for more shine. And although it is an investment (the handle is hand-finished), this is a tool you’ll use for years to come.

Bristle type: boar, nylonBarrel material: Italian resinSize options: medium, large

If you're in search of a round brush with frizz-control benefits, look no further than this top-rated, ergonomic favorite from Drybar. After all, who better to go to than the blowout blueprint? Its bristles are optimized with ionic technology to mitigate frizz while providing salon-level tension control. It even has a thumbprint grip to help maximize your styling experience.

Bristle type: boarBarrel material: ceramicSize options: medium, large

If you're already a fan of Moroccanoil's coveted hair staples, allow us to introduce you to its top-rated ceramic and ion-infused round brush. It heats up when in use with a blow-dryer, making drying and detangling hair a breeze. Designed for medium to long hair, the large barrel covers a good amount of surface area without tugging. Plus, the ceramic finish leaves hair extra-shiny.

Bristle type: nylon, ionicBarrel material: ceramicSize options: 55 millimeters, 45 millimeters

This $14 round brush has earned a five-star rating from beauty lovers. The bristles are heat-resistant vegan boar bristles, and they're pretty densely packed, so you can get a grip and lift on the hair while still allowing strands to glide through without pulling. The bristles also aren’t overly stiff, so they won’t scratch your scalp as you’re styling. And we’re always here for a no-slip grip!

Bristle type: vegan boarBarrel material: bambooSize options: 2.78 inches

These U.K.-made brushes are celebrity stylist Benjamin Mohapi’s personal favorites. “They are the oldest brush company in the world, and I've used them for the entire 40 years of my career,” he explains, adding that the brand has options in boar bristle, vegan bristle, and nylon ceramic. “As far as I’m concerned, there is nothing better.”

Bristle type: nylonBarrel material: ceramicSize options: 45mm and 60mm

Here's a brush from the experts at Sisley Paris that's literally made for your next blowout. Its boar and nylon bristles are gentle yet effective, and compactly housed in a wooden barrel. The soft bristles provide hold for easy drying and also keep the hair super soft. We also appreciate that it has scalp-stimulating bristles, so it feels especially nice when you use it at the root.

Bristle type: boar, nylonBarrel material: woodSize options: medium

This popular round brush proves that getting a polished, salon-level finish doesn't have to cost a ton. Ideal for reducing frizz, the staple uses a ceramic-coated barrel for even heat distribution. Plus, it features an ergonomic handle for extra comfort, which you’ll love whether you’re new to blowing out your hair at home or just need the extra grip. Once you get the hang of things, you may decide to upgrade, but it’s a great starter brush.

Bristle type: nylonBarrel material: ceramicSize options: 1.7 inches, 1.3 inches

The three sturdy knobs on the handle of this round brush mean you don't have to worry about the tool accidentally slipping out of your hands mid-blow-dry. Plus, the placement of the bristles in Philip B.'s tool makes it easy to give your roots an extra lift. With a mix of boar bristles and nylon for extra reinforcement, it’s a sturdy brush that’s earned stellar reviews for good reason.

Bristle type: boar, nylonBarrel material: mahogany Size options: large, medium

Can a round brush be chic? This one could be mistaken for a found object with its teak handle. Simmonds recommends the Japanese-made brand, saying that it offers “quality brushes that deliver results, look great coming out of my kit, and last for ages with proper care.” The mix of boar bristle and heat-resistant nylon pins is also ideal for optimal hold and shine.

Bristle type: boar, heat-resistant nylonBarrel material: teak woodSize options: several

Balmain Hair may sound like a fun splurge (and it is), but Simmonds says the brand has become a go-to for his ceramic tools. “Their range is every bit as luxurious as you would expect,” he says. This brush in particular helps seal the cuticle for an extra-smooth and shiny blowout, and can even work for extra-long hair. Have short hair? Try the 25mm version for the same luxury experience.

Bristle type: syntheticBarrel material: ceramic coatingSize options: 25mm–53mm

Like anything worth learning, styling your hair with one of the best round brushes takes practice. "Ask your stylist if they’ll do a lesson," offers Gamez. It also might be helpful to reference a YouTube or TikTok tutorial. The general guidance is: start fresh out of the shower, detangle your hair, and then begin blow-drying and brushing small sections of hair, working your way around your head. You can start in the front or back sections. Professional hair colorist Tylor Johnson recommends starting with the front sections around your hairline, as your hairline pieces are the most important and require the most precision, and you don't want to be fatigued when styling them.

What Are the Benefits of Using a Round Brush and Blow-Dryer?

It’s all about the shape. “The round brush allows the hair to wrap around it, creating a bend in the hair as it dries and cools,” explains Mohapi, who explains that a regular hairbrush should not be used for heat-styling. “Expensive brushes like Masons are not designed for direct heat, and you will ruin a perfectly beautiful brush,” says the stylist.

Several top stylists prefer to use round brushes and blow-dryers separately rather than an all-in-one device, as it enhances control while minimizing hair damage. "Blow-dry brushes tend to be high heat, so if you’re putting that tool on soaking wet hair, you’re going to overdry and damage it," explains Gamez. "A blow-dryer allows you to create space between the heat and the hair shaft as needed. You can control the air direction of a dryer with your nozzle, pointing downward and sealing the cuticle, resulting in shinier, smoother hair, as opposed to a hot brush that blows air outward in all directions."

On the other hand, blow-dry round brushes definitely have their benefits. According to Valles, one key factor is their overall ease of use to craft salon-worthy styles at home. "A blow-dry brush makes the process so much easier," Valles says. "Some people aren't as coordinated handling a brush and dryer simultaneously, but the blow-dry brush gives the same results and is easier to manage." At the end of the day, the best round brush for you is one you actually enjoy using regularly.

“Many people wouldn’t want to use a boar bristle brush for ethical reasons, and there are lots of good alternatives, so boar bristle isn’t the only option,” explains Mohapi. “It is, however, a highly effective material and is used extensively in hairbrush manufacturing globally.” Here’s why: “Boar bristles are highly tolerant of heat and won’t melt or buckle under the intense extremes of a packed Friday at the salon. The fibers are both strong and flexible and have the unique quality of being able to almost grab the hair and hold it in place as you pass it through the brush.”

That said, the stylist adds that some synthetics are nearly as good. They just may not last as long. “Nylon is a different thing altogether,” he notes. “Nylon will close down the cuticle and help create a shiny, slick finish to the hair. This is great for straight hair, but nylon brushes take all the body out of the hair, so when you use a nylon round brush, it has a different type of finish.”

Of course, that means the cast is twice as packed with familiar faces (including Cosmo’s latest cover stars Whitney Peak, Mckenna Grace, and Maya Hawke). Wondering who from the star-studded cast is taken IRL? Keep scrolling to meet the real-life partners of The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping cast, from Joseph Zada’s lowkey girlfriend Polly Talarico to Mckenna Grace’s adorable showmance with Mason Thames.

#6828. Dressing Like Your Boyfriend Is Actually Fine

Take more, give less in the relationship, ladies.

Is it so wrong to dress like your boyfriend? Clearly, the internet thinks so. Most recently, Bella Hadid has found herself at the center of this conversation, with people highlighting her various boyfriend eras like some sort of sartorial identity crisis. From the emo-adjacent vibes when she dated the singer, The Weeknd, to the cowboy-rodeo-chic aesthetic with Adan Banuelos, and now her hockey-game-attending, matching outfit moment with new man Nathan Lacy, Bella has seemingly been accused of basing her clothes after her romantic partners.

I personally think it’s a silly argument given that Bella Hadid is one of the most notable supermodels with an eccentric, well-documented high-fashion sense of style. She was awarded Lyst’s 2022 Most Powerful Dresser, and she famously styles much of her own day-to-day looks. So who cares if, during a particular season of her life, she wants to tap into the aesthetic of the man she’s dating?

The same argument has been made about Ariana Grande, Kim Kardashian, and, more recently (even by her own sisters, who do the same thing), Kourtney Kardashian. Ariana’s personal style has never particularly screamed fashion girl to me, and I’m a huge proponent of the idea that not everyone needs to be one anyway. Grande works with stylists to build her looks, so if on her off days she wants to dress in tandem with her beau, there’s truly nothing wrong with that.

Kim Kardashian, meanwhile, notoriously skyrocketed into the high-fashion space during her relationship with Kanye West. To this day, Kim gives style credits to Kanye’s eye for fashion and his introducing her to the world she so desperately wanted to be part of. Did she take what he taught her and expand upon it, eventually establishing herself as a fashion mogul in her own right? Yes. So technically in the long term, borrowing an aesthetic from her boyfriend then husband clearly worked out for her.

And while I personally wouldn’t wear Kourtney’s ultra-rock-girlfriend aesthetic, I actually think it's fun. After spending a decade being terrorized by Scott Disick (terrible boyfriend, excellent family friend to her sisters), I think it’s perfectly fine for my girl to enjoy someone so much she clearly feels safe enough to wrap herself in his sartorial aesthetic.

This idea that women are in the wrong for “adopting” their man’s fashion seems uninteresting to me. If anything, women should be stealing men’s aesthetics and making them their own more often. There's something cheeky about the whole thing, a small flip of patriarchy on its head. How often do we see the internet go viral over the invisible labor women put into the men they date? Making their style better, recommending the haircut that best frames their face, teaching them how to groom themselves. It’s always give, give, give. So why not take something too? Because the next video down my timeline after those labor of love, “girlfriend effect” ones is women complaining about men taking everything they gave in a relationship (from hair and clothing reccs) and bringing it to the next woman.

There's something rather liberating about having your own style to return to while allowing yourself to experiment with whatever your boyfriend of the moment is into. It’s like playing dress-up. You’re enjoying a man, his world, and even his wardrobe, without having to make any of it your permanent identity—simply trying it out like free samples of ice cream. And when he’s no longer serving you, you move on. Thank you, next, as Miss Grande says.

Because women are so often expected to do the emotional and personal-care work in relationships, it’s honestly fine to me seeing these celebrities lean into their boyfriends’ fashion sense. (Side note: Incredible that these men have a style at all—it can be sartorially bleak out there!) Maybe his hockey jerseys look good on you. Maybe his leather jackets are better than yours. Maybe you just like the way you look in a cowboy hat right now.

It isn’t hurting anyone. And if anything, the most radical part of all is letting the men, for once, shape the fashion in their relationship. High time we all start letting them handle the burden of lessening the swag gap.

Aiyana Ishmael is the style editor at Cosmopolitan magazine. In her work, Aiyana focuses on the culture of fashion and how it intertwines and shapes the zeitgeist. She is an award-winning journalist from Miami, Florida, and a graduate of the historically Black university, Florida A&M. She is a 2024 Forbes 30 Under 30: Media honoree, and her debut romance novella PASSING GAME is set to release March of 2027 (831 Stories/Simon & Schuster).

From silk pants to slouchy trousers, there’s something for everyone

There’s nothing quite as chic as having good travel style. While we’ve certainly moved on from the days of wearing pencil skirts and heels to walk the glossy airport corridors (thank goodness), jet-set style has evolved into something a little more manageable and comfortable.

These days, sneakers and flats run the show in terms of footwear, but where it gets really interesting is in the pants.

It girls like Gigi Hadid and Kendall Jenner tend to lean into model-off-duty attire in cozy knit bottoms (paired with luxury designer bags) for their busy travel days. Others like Jennifer Lopez, Julia Roberts, and Amal Clooney remain loyal to their jeans, opting for comfortable styles that can support them through the airport chaos.

For effortless travel style, celebrities like Jennifer Lawrence, Zoë Kravitz, and Rihanna have a soft spot for the chic pull-on pant. It’s easy, sleek, and practical all in one. What more could you want?

#6817. Rhode’s Annual Birthday Collection Is Here, and It Features the Brand’s First-Ever Apparel

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Being the giving Sagittarius she is, Hailey Bieber actually gives us something every year for her birthday: a new Rhode collection. Sometimes it’s a special-edition shade or flavor of her award-winning Peptide Lip Tint. Other years, it’s a brand-new product, like the Peptide Eye Prep Hydrogel Patches and a new snap-on lip case. But the 1996-er went all out for her 30th birthday, dropping new products, exclusive styles and colors on some of the brand’s go-to items, and even its first-ever apparel.

Today, Rhode announced Pocket Plush, the brand’s new matte blush in two shades: Baldwin, a berry rose that’s allegedly Hailey’s go-to shade, and Tucson, a rich burgundy. For now, it comes in a limited-edition mini navy packaging that’s v cute (and has been teased for a while on Rhode’s IG). What makes it different from Pocket Blush? Well, the creamy stick is said to leave a velvety, soft matte finish on skin. Think blurring and smoothing, not dewy and fresh like the OG. You can also wear them on cheeks and lips, which Hailey loves to pair together for a monochromatic look.

But that’s not even the most exciting new launch, IMO. For the first time ever, Rhode is launching apparel. It’s only on pre-order right now, but the HB Windbreaker was inspired by Hailey’s love of vintage sportswear. It’s an easy “throw on and go,” oversized jacket that can be worn two ways. There’s navy nylon on one side and gray fleece on the other. If you wanted to match your Rhode phone case, lip balm, and Glazing Milk, now’s your chance. I’m pretty confident this one will sell out, especially ahead of gifting season.

Oh! And that’s not all! (Told ya, Hailey went all out this year.) There’s a limited-edition flavor of the Peptide Lip Boost, a rich, moisturizing lip mask that Cosmo editors have loved since it launched. This one has a shimmery sheer pink tint and a cherry vanilla cola scent. Sounds delicious.

Plus, there are exclusive styles of the Peptide Eye Prep, both in navy to match the collection. You can also grab the Rhode Carry Case and Snap-On Lip Case, both now available in navy just for Hailey’s bday.

Of course, if you wanted to pick up a set to gift (ahem, as a fellow Sag, I can confirm anyone wIth a bday coming up soon would love it), grab The Birthday Set: it contains both shades of Pocket Plush and the Peptide Lip Boost in Cherry Vanilla Cola.

Everything launches (including the windbreaker preorder) on Friday, November 20, at 9 a.m. PT. As all Rhode things go, these will absolutely sell out quickly. If you have your eye on something, I wouldn’t wait.

#6814. This London Townhouse Was Designed for the Holidays

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE Decor editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Miles Redd and David Kaihoi created the ultimate English Christmas retreat for longtime clients—cozy, festive, and swathed in reds and greens.

Above: In the living room, the seating is by Luther Quintana with Samuel & Sons fringe. The wallcovering is by Iksel, and the cocktail table has a Fornasetti top.

Some homes are for summers by the water, others for weekends on the slopes. When Krystyna Houser set about redecorating her family’s London townhouse, she was thinking of Christmas. “We spend every Christmas there,” she says. At this house, that meant more than just putting up a tree.

Located near Richmond Park, in southwestern London, the 19th-century townhouse has been in Houser’s family for more than 20 years. It was always a place to return to while Houser, founder of the nutritional startup mBIOTA Elemental, and her husband Dan, a co-founder of Rockstar Games, were raising their three children in New York City. Dan had grown up nearby, and the ample greenspace that gives the area its name made it an appealing place to visit with a young family. The interiors, however, had never quite received their due.

Houser turned to Miles Redd and David Kaihoi, the partners behind the New York–based firm Redd Kaihoi. She had known Redd since 2012, when she hired him to decorate a Brooklyn Heights townhouse, and the two became friends. They have since worked on multiple houses together. For London she trusted the designers to craft the warm and celebratory atmosphere she craved. “I wanted it to be equally elegant, cozy, and family-oriented,” she says.

Red and green were a natural starting point, as green already had the family’s allegiance. “It’s one of their favorite colors,” Redd says. “So we leaned into that a lot.” Verdant hues appear in velvet, felt, and marble throughout, while the entrance hall is dressed in crimson. These were holiday colors with no intention of coming down in January, and Redd was going for sophistication rather than holiday kitsch. “I wanted romantic 19th-century elegance, the feeling that they’re stepping into a Merchant Ivory movie.”

This approach suits a firm known for exuberant color and layered furnishings. Redd brings a sense of theater to traditional decorating, drawing on the work of such legendary designers as Billy Baldwin, Albert Hadley, and John Fowler and mixing lacquer, chintz, and bold geometric patterns. Before opening his practice in 1998, he trained with antiques dealer John Rosselli and decorator Bunny Williams. Kaihoi, a sculptor, joined the ELLE Decor A-List firm in 2008 and became a partner in 2019. Together the duo give grand rooms an ease and wit that keep formality in check.

Houser considers “interior designer” an insufficient description for Redd and Kaihoi. “I feel like they are set curators,” she says. She gave them considerable freedom, occasionally inviting them to surprise her with potential purchases. “I don’t think it’s my place to hinder creative expression, and, as a result, the rooms are just magical.”

The house’s existing architecture gave that freedom some useful structure. “It had great English bones,” Redd says. He retained the original living room mantelpiece, library doors, and front door, while upstairs he and Kaihoi worked to restore character lost to earlier alterations that had stripped away 19th-century details. An excavation created additional basement space for a screening room and a ping-pong area, giving the family more places to gather and play.

From first visit to completion, the project took the better part of an unhurried decade, with pauses for permits, the Covid pandemic, and other commitments along the way. All the while Redd and Kaihoi kept shopping at auctions, antiques stores, and fairs, which ensured that the rooms would have an accumulated character. The designers looked to the guards at Buckingham Palace for the entrance hall and main staircase, borrowing the red and gold of their uniforms for its matka silk walls trimmed with gold braiding. Beyond the hall, the green velvet–lined library opens into a living room papered to resemble a coromandel screen.

Upstairs, the primary bedroom and a sitting area occupy a long, narrow space. Green felt screen dividers give the bed its own area in one corner without a wall breaking up the suite. “It’s all sort of one big bedsit, which is a very English notion,” Redd says. A small painting of a parrot on one of the screens keeps Houser company at her bedside.

The couple wanted their suite to feel like a luxurious hotel—inviting places to read and work, a comfortable bed, and a generously scaled bathroom—without sacrificing a sense of home. “You’ve ruined hotels for us,” Redd recalls Houser telling him of their previous work.

Step Inside the Ultimate Christmas Retreat

But Houser’s favorite rooms are the ones that bring everyone together: the kitchen and the adjoining striped family room, which open onto the garden. It’s here where everyone gathers for meals and to relax. The connected spaces provide separate places to eat, play games, and watch television, allowing the family to spend time together even when everyone is doing something different.

“Their design helps bring us together,” Houser says. “And that’s what it is. It’s a family home.”

This story originally appeared in the November 2026 issue of ELLE Decor.

I’ll be doing my holiday shopping early with this sale.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Is there anything Shark Beauty can’t do? The brand has mastered the art of making high-tech beauty tools that deliver seriously impressive results at a fraction of the price of its competitors. #6806. Léa Seydoux Isn’t Trying to Act. She’s Trying to Live

The French actress continues her string of emotionally demanding roles in The Unknown and Gentle Monster

Léa Seydoux first stepped into the limelight in Christophe Honoré’s The Beautiful Person, where her role as Junie de Chartres earned her the Trophée Chopard award for rising actors at Cannes. From there, she moved seamlessly between highbrow French cinema and Hollywood, achieving a critical breakthrough with Blue Is the Warmest Color in 2013 and starring alongside Daniel Craig as a Bond girl in Spectre and No Time to Die. Most years, she’s starred in at least two films, and 2026 is no exception: She plays a woman with a man’s soul trapped in her body in Arthur Harari’s psychological fantasy The Unknown, and in Marie Kreutzer’s Gentle Monster, releasing on Netflix on November 18, she’s a pianist who unearths her husband’s hideous crimes. The words thorny and barbed read like understatements for both films, and it’s exactly what interests Seydoux about the stories. Like in life itself, she avoids easy answers, opting instead for exploration.

Seydoux got the chance to explore an especially ambitious idea in The Unknown. It tells the story of David Zimmerman (Niels Schneider), a photographer who wakes up in the body of a woman he sleeps with. Seydoux plays both that woman, Eva, and the man trapped within her body. “I felt the movie was something new to me, to play an impossible idea and make it believable, try to give it a truthful dimension,” Seydoux says. She’s no stranger to high-concept stories, previously starring in Bertrand Bonello’s The Beast and David Cronenberg’s Crimes of the Future. Each time, she imbues these characters with humanity, despite their far-fetched sci-fi dilemmas.

“I like movies that make you think, movies that bring up questions, when there’s a debate around the film,” she says. “What I love the most is to explore human nature, our human condition. This is what cinema allows us to do.”

Seydoux might vehemently love movies, yet acting remains a demanding process for her. She relies on the art form to dig into feelings and emotions. “I don’t feel necessarily very comfortable with acting. It’s not a place where I’m like, ‘I love it,’ but at the same time, I find it a bit painful. It’s more a way to experience things and ideas,” she says.

Gentle Monster provides another knotty premise, this time grounded in reality. It follows Lucy Weiss, a famed experimental pianist who gives up her career to raise her young son with her doting husband, Philip (Laurence Rupp). One day, the police knock on their door, and it’s revealed that Philip is accused of distributing child pornography.

“The subject matter is tough, but the fact that it’s through the woman’s perspective—and not through the victim or perpetrator—it’s through her emotions that the story unfolds [is what interested me],” she says. “Things are not too defined, but it’s also because of the subject matter; it was the only way to talk about it. [Marie] gave me a lot of freedom.”

Born in Paris in 1985, Seydoux spent her childhood wedged among seven siblings and half-siblings. She remembers feeling isolated, practically invisible, as a child. “I was always in the shadows in a way,” she says. “As a kid, I felt transparent. I was never at the center of [my] big family.” This plays a big part in her surprise at her acting career, never thinking of herself as someone deserving of center stage, of fame, of attention. In fact, acting never even crossed her mind until she befriended some actors when she turned 18. “I loved the way they lived their lives,” she says. “I admired their life—the fact that they were free.”

And yet, she rejects the label of “actress.” “I try to be truthful as much as possible,” she says. “I try not to act. This is what I try to be, and I’m more into reaction than action.” She adds, “It’s more reactive than acting.”

For Seydoux, “reacting” and really experiencing life is only possible if she “abandons” herself to her characters. One of the most intense examples of this is Abdellatif Kechiche’s controversial lesbian love story Blue Is the Warmest Color, in which she stars alongside Adèle Exarchopoulos. The film’s schedule stretched from two months to five months, and it has since become notorious for its grueling pace. (The famous sex scene took 10 days alone.) “The greatest and most intense experience I’ve had was with Adèle when we did Blue Is the Warmest Color,” she recalls. “It was crazy, and really the connection between us had to be very, very intense. I think, with Adèle, it’s something that will always be a part of me now. There was an exchange, and it was more than making a film. It was really a life experience.”

One scene from Gentle Monster lingered with me for weeks after I watched it: Lucy’s snotty, tearful confrontation with her husband at their family home as she pleads with him to tell the truth. This close-up of Seydoux not only accentuates her famous blue eyes but also shows her uncanny control of this character’s emotions. It feels, for lack of better words, real. Seydoux partly credits her empathy to being a mother of two children, but this scene goes beyond compassion; it shows how much of herself she gives to the character. Before filming this scene, Kreutzer offered little direction, letting her tap into how the character felt. “I prepare myself, and I have to just let myself go and be in the moment and just open up,” Seydoux says, suggesting that perhaps these emotions otherwise lie dormant within her.

I found this is what Seydoux means by “reaction.” Every role in her varied and extolled filmography functions as an avenue through which she can explore what it means to be human, what it means to feel. Her natural curiosity about the world and its inhabitants existed before she decided to act in her teens and will exist long into the future, even if acting jobs don’t remain on the table. “If it ended tomorrow, I’d be happy,” she says. “I will find a way. Now, I really feel that I’m acting because I still have the opportunity. But I have more things to explore, and I would love to have more experiences.”

#6805. 12 12 12 Shakes Up the Tired Heist Formula with One Great Idea

Can the Anthony Mackie-led crime drama pull it off?

“A high-asset robbery, professionally conceived, is twelve months in planning, twelve hours in execution,” says Anthony Mackie’s character, FBI special agent Jack Strickland, at the start of the new Apple TV crime drama, 12 12 12. “A bank robber who is not caught within twelve days is not caught at all.”

It’s an amazing premise—honestly, one of the best I’ve heard in a long time. The first episode takes the viewer one month into planning the heist, one day into pulling off the heist, and one hour into their attempted escape. Then, episode 2 will move along to two months, two days, and two hours. Neat, right? But the big question for cocreators Jim Keeble and Dudi Appleton’s time-shifting heist series remains the same as any seemingly impossible bank job: Do they have the right team to pull it off?

From the two-episode premiere—now available to stream on Apple TV—I’d say we’re off to a very strong start. 12 12 12’s opening scene pays homage to Ocean’s 11’s Shaobo Qin when a contortionist unfolds his body and climbs out of a large safety deposit box. It’s one of the most genius ideas I’ve seen in a heist story of the last few years—I mean, talk about a man on the inside—especially in an overcrowded field of complex heist series. Remember Netflix’s Kaleidoscope, which allowed viewers to watch the story starting on any episode in the series? Well, 12 12 12 is far more than just a gimmick.

The story follows FBI special agent Jack Strickland (Mackie), who has tracked down one of the world’s best bank robbers, Paul Galvin (Jamie Dornan), to Zurich, Switzerland. Galvin and his unruly brother Bobby (Jack Kesy, who was last seen playing Robbie Pedergrast’s late brother on Task), made an enemy of Strickland when the FBI agent’s partner was killed as she attempted to arrest them during a major heist in Newark, New Jersey.

Now, Strickland has a personal vendetta against the man. He throws his philosophy that you’ll never catch a bank robber after twelve days of the robbery out the window—which is fine because the creators fabricated the whole “12 12 12” FBI rule anyway just to provide episodic framing for the series. (Maybe it resets now that Galvin is planning another heist?) Either way, 12 12 12 does a great job of making sure you never get lost in the three timelines.

Strickland’s white whale is also far from a one-note character himself. He’s a man following in his father’s footsteps to get out of a life of poverty, which would be easier if his brother Bobby wasn’t such of a wild card who can’t be trusted unattended for a single second.

As it turns out, Bobby gambled away all their money from the Newark heist, including Paul’s half. So his brother’s debtors force Paul back into a life of crime under threat of violence to his new lady love in France, his father-in-law, and their unborn child. It’s also quickly revealed that our contortionist is just one of the interesting members of Paul’s team, who are all tasked by Bobby’s mysterious benefactors with robbing a Zurich bank of 800,000 Swiss francs all owned by the Russian mafia.

From the first two episodes released so far, the 12 12 12 team seems like it has the right men for the job. The timeline-shifting format allows the series to do much more than simply break the action to provide a little backstory. As we move from months ahead to days after, and vice-versa, we also switch perspectives that allow for Paul and Jack to trade who is the hero and who is the villain.

Jack can be both the righteous FBI agent pursuing the bank robbers in the present and a man clearly far too hellbent on revenge in the past. The same goes for Paul, whose team blackmails, honeypots, and occasionally murders throughout the heist, while his backstory paints him as a good man who was forced down a dark road.

Where 12 12 12 falls short, at least so far, is in the script. Kari Skogland (who previously worked with Mackie on The Falcon and the Winter Soldier) directs the two-part premiere handily enough, but Keeble and Appleton’s writing is very formulaic. Scripts don’t always matter when you’re working with amazing characters like the contortionist lockpick. True Detective: Night Country’s Kali Reis is also on the team. Her mission is to hack into the subway system so that they can make their escape on public transit, which, of course, goes wrong. But when you have so much dialogue doubling as exposition in between the action, it feels like everyone’s conversations are more for the viewer than they are for each other.

For now, I’m hopeful that all the kinks are worked out once we move past the who, what, and why of 12 12 12’s opening episodes. Given the gimmick, and the fact that the series is only eight episodes instead of twelve, I’m excited to see just what throws the entire concept of the series into chaos.
