#6803. A Menorca Farmhouse Finds Its Easy Elegance

Isabel López-Quesada honored the historical essence of a 19th-century Menorcan retreat while introducing vibrant colors and an elevated ease.

Above: In the living room, López-Quesada replicated the original ceiling beams and finished the walls in traditional Menorcan lime plaster. A sofa of her design is grouped with a 1960s Tito Agnoli rattan daybed, a Mexican palm leaf stool, and a custom cocktail table upholstered with a Moroccan rug.

For Spanish designer Isabel López-Quesada, summers tend to revolve around Biarritz, in southwestern France, where she has a home. Still, she always carves out time for Menorca, regarded as the most laid-back of Spain’s Balearic Islands. That vibe suits the ELLE Decor A-list designer, whose signature style is luxurious but unfussy, harmonizing old and new with breezy refinement.

Typically, López-Quesada goes there to relax and spend time with friends, but around five years ago she received a call from a Bilbao family who asked her to refresh their vacation home outside Mahón, Menorca’s capital. An old farm property, or finca, the five-bedroom residence required extensive updates. López-Quesada was steadfast in preserving such historical elements as a well and donkey stables, which she transformed into an indoor-outdoor bathroom with a patio and marble tub. “It was important to respect the style of Menorca,” she says. “We were very careful with the materials, so it all goes together and conveys the sentiment I want the house to have.”

López-Quesada founded her namesake studio as a precocious 20-year-old Madrileña 40 years ago. Initially inspired by her mother’s yen for decorating and the work of celebrated Spanish designers such as Paco Muñoz and Fernando Benjumea, López-Quesada garnered wide attention in the early 2000s, when she converted an old Madrid wax factory into an atelier and a luxurious home for her family, filled with antiques and contemporary pieces. Soon she had interest from clients all over the world. Today López-Quesada’s 12-person team includes one of her three adult children, daughter Isabel Llanza. Five years ago the pair co-founded the lifestyle brand Isita Home, selling products they make themselves or discover on their travels.

The Menorca project began by gutting the house’s interior and rebuilding it “in an old style,” López-Quesada says. Central to it all was adding custom windows and doors to each room so the island’s golden light could flood the spaces. Vaulted and beamed ceilings were left intact or were recreated. Reconfiguring the floor plan, López-Quesada eschewed a proper dining room in favor of an eat-in open kitchen. “Most of the time you are eating outside,” she says.

With Menorca’s unrelenting summer sun, bougainvillea-draped pergolas were a must for leisurely alfresco lunches and dinners. Swaths of white lantana and lily of the Nile border the terraces, while “the pool reflects the sky like a mirror and provides a refreshing feel,” López-Quesada says.

Her collaborator on the gardens was Spanish landscape architect Luis González-Camino. “My vision was to design a water-efficient Mediterranean garden, divided into distinct yet connected ‘garden rooms’ for different seasons and times of day,” he says. González-Camino preserved the property as a natural Mediterranean woodland, with wild olive trees providing cover.

Indoors, López-Quesada installed French terra-cotta floor tiles chosen for their aged look. The three fireplaces were all handcrafted in traditional Menorcan style—a little imperfect, painted white—and decorated with vintage tiles the designer keeps in her Madrid warehouse.

She offset the white ceilings and lime plaster walls with vibrant textiles and tilework in blues and greens inspired by Menorca’s native palette. An upstairs bedroom is anchored by an iron four-poster bed with a blue-and-white-striped canopy; its bath has a shower lined with lapis-colored zellige tiles. Doors, which across Menorca are customarily painted in bold shades, here are finished in verdant hues.

Custom cabinets are designed with whimsically patterned cutouts for airflow, since humidity is a major factor. “On the islands, if you close the cupboards completely, mushrooms will grow inside,” López-Quesada says.

Step Inside This Renovated Menorca Retreat

She arrayed the spaces with a lively mix of vintage and new furnishings, emphasizing organic materials: rattan chairs, bamboo shades, Moroccan rugs, lots of linen and cotton fabrics in spirited florals or stripes. “If I use a heavy piece, then the rest is lighter,” she says. The tableware maintains the blue and green theme, including pieces from Isita Home and hand-blown glassware from neighboring Mallorca.

In the end, the owners asked López-Quesada to redo their home in Spain’s Basque region as well. It’s one of several projects she currently has across the country, including reimagining a hotel in Barcelona, and she’s also designing homes in Paris, Shanghai, and the Dominican Republic. Many of her projects are with repeat clients. “That’s how it works,” the designer says. “It’s like a chain.”

This story originally appeared in the November 2026 issue of ELLE Decor.

Sarah Cristobal is a writer, editor, and creative consultant. She is currently editor-at-large for 10 Magazine USA, among other freelance projects.

#6801. Elias Matso Is the Twisted Corsetier Dressing Sydney Sweeney and Jenna Ortega

The young designer talked to Bazaar about craftsmanship, fittings, and developing his signature technique

These days, Elias Matso is known for twisted corsetry worn by the likes of Sydney Sweeney and Jenna Ortega. But the first time he noticed the impact of a good costume, he was a kid watching Julie Andrews in Victor/Victoria. He sat by the TV watching the Oscar-winning actor perform “Le Jazz Hot!” in Art Deco gowns, decadent headpieces, and dramatic feathered capes. “I know she was acting in that scene, but I just felt like she was so happy to be wearing those costumes,” he tells Harper’s Bazaar. “And I was obsessed with that.”

Matso is now 24, and it’s easy to see the impact of that moment on his creative approach. A recent graduate of Polimoda in Florence with Charles James, Bob Mackie, Mugler, and Mr. Pearl on his mood board, he crafts avant-garde, couture corsetry. (It’s perhaps no surprise that he has a history of making costumes for drag performers.) His approach is personal and intimate, rooted in hand craftsmanship and collaborative dialogue with clients.

In October 2025, Sweeney made waves when she wore a crystal naked dress, pulled from Christian Cowan’s Spring/Summer 2026 collection and made in collaboration with the young designer. The piece featured twisted corsetry and a semi-sheer body that hugged the Euphoria star’s body—and, naturally, the public had quite a lot to say about it. “I did not anticipate that it would be as big as it was,” the artist admits. “It was really cool that the conversation left fashion and became a bigger conversation about women’s bodies and what modesty should look like or shouldn’t look like. And how a lot of the world doesn’t know what boobs look like.”

While the discourse surprised Matso, it reflected one of his own core beliefs: that “fashion is at least 50% the person who’s wearing the thing.” He keeps this in mind during fittings, ensuring clients feel comfortable and beautiful every step of the way. Below, he talks to Bazaar about his early references, his focus on craftsmanship, and the colorful steel-boning dress recently worn by Jenna Ortega.

As a kid, I had a paper doll of myself, and whenever there were clothes that I wanted that I didn’t have, I would make them for the paper doll. It was pocket sized, and whenever I was bored—which was all the time, because I have attention deficit issues—I would pull it out and play.

It is. It was my third year and I wanted to explore the tension that children hold, when they feel like they’re growing faster than they can keep up with, and feeling at odds with themselves, because that’s how I felt when I was a kid. There was this feeling, especially in the queer world, of being pulled in different directions, gender-wise. And so I had developed that idea, and then through sketching and conversations with my teachers, we started getting more and more towards this very literal idea of a twist. And then the collaboration that I did with Christian Cowan pushed it further.

I was in New York over the summer before my senior year, and I was looking for jobs and not getting anything. I started looking into scooping ice cream at Van Leeuwen’s and stuff like that. And then, one day, I got a text from Alicia Keys’s stylist and a DM from Christian that was like, “Do you want to do some dresses for my show?” It was a good day.

It actually wasn’t originally supposed to be that fabric. We couldn’t find the fabric we wanted, and we couldn’t figure out how to put as many stones as we needed onto the fabric that we found. Then we just found this sheer fabric, and we were like, “Okay, it’s going to be the original dress, but it’s going to be completely see-through.”

I made it to fit this gorgeous six-foot-tall model from Finland. And this is why fit is so fun, because with that dress, there was this miracle where it fit her so perfectly one way, and then it fit Sydney, who has completely different proportions, in this totally other, also beautiful way. Often when things get pulled from a runway, they don’t fit like that. And it was just really cool to see.

I really like meeting people in person when I can because it just always creates a better outcome. I feel like the conversation is so much about your personality, but also your physiology, the way you’re built and the way you’re made up. People’s bodies respond differently. So it’s really important to do fittings and to check in with people before, during, and after the fitting, and ask, “How intense of an experience do you want this to be? What will make you feel the most beautiful, the most comfortable?”

Sometimes a little bit of discomfort is part of being comfortable, but you have to have signed up for that. That’s my thing that I’ve learned: Discomfort, if you’re prepared, can be part of it. But there has to be a conversation if discomfort is involved, or else we are forgetting that fashion is at least 50% the person who’s wearing the thing.

When I start a collection, I’m always looking through the archives of a museum, like the Whitney or often the Guggenheim, because you have less control over what you’re going to find. [For this piece,] I had found one painting that led me to Mark Ranger’s website. And we got in touch and then collaborated the whole year. It turns out we explore a lot of the same themes. Here, the dress was supposed to be about time and our body’s relationship to gravity and this idea of melting, not in a way that we’re trying to resist and slow down, but in a way that’s just natural and beautiful.

The goal was to make something unrecognizable, but using recognizable techniques. I already had a lot of steel boning because it’s the same stuff that’s inside all these corsets. And so I just got more, and then I started chopping it up into smaller and smaller pieces. I was able to create a relationship with Preciosa, who does crystals for a lot of big brands—I met them through a trip that they did to my school. I just came up with this weird technique that was, I guess, a test of my own physical and mental strength in a way.

I didn’t make it with Jenna in mind. I never thought it would have all these different lives and stages.

I guess I’m seeing it less and less valued in fashion. And even when something is hand-done and comes out gorgeous and successful, the people that wind up getting the praise for those things are not the people who do the thing, but the people who have curated those artisans—the artistic directors, the designers. My friends and I have just graduated, we’re looking for work, and I see my friends who have really singular skills getting so undervalued for their work and their time. That is something that is really frustrating to me. And in an age where so many things can be falsified via technology and the internet, I think things that you can’t fake are how something’s made, how something stands up over time, how something feels on the inside when you put it on. And designers care less and less about that, I guess.

I’m in a transitional stage where I’m not sure what I’m gonna do next. Every day, I get people asking me to make dresses on this scale for free or for bafflingly low amounts of money. You know, I’m told all the time that there’s no budget for things. People want couture for no budget. People in this industry have no shame when it comes to asking for that. For emerging designers, there are so many of us that they know that if I can’t supply what they’re looking for, they will find another independent designer who has the resources or the funds or the ability to provide that thing for less than what it’s worth. I do worry about that. But this press is making me think that maybe there is a niche for something that walks the line between art and fashion and not in the aesthetic sense, which people talk about all the time, but in the financial and logistical sense. So that’s what I’m trying to figure out.

Also, I found that if people are asking for things for free, it’s very rarely the client themselves. It’s often their team. I love the art of styling, but that is another reason that I find one-on-one relationships and conversations with the person who you’re going to be dressing really important. It makes them understand the level of attention or care that they are interacting with.

This is still in process, but I’m working on a piece with Laverne Cox. I always wanted to work with her. And then starting to work with her, I was like, “I was right.” Because she has such appreciation and respect for the process of everything and the details and how everything works. She’s a really respectful and engaging patron, and we’ve had a lot of interesting conversations about the designers that we both look up to.

For me, I want to take the momentum that I have and do something that feels exciting with it. And at the same time, I am not sure that I have the experience to start a company, you know? I am in conversations with lots of people who I’ve always wanted to collaborate with, and it’s so exciting. Really, really exciting and mind-blowing. And at the same time, I’m reaching out to brands that I admire to see what openings they have because we all need stability. I’m trying to remain as flexible as possible.

#6798. The Design Fiend’s Guide to Tangier

The Moroccan city, a complex, culturally kaleidoscopic crossroads between Europe and Africa, is home to a savvy community of creatives whose stylish impact continues to grow. Word is out.

Above: The pool terrace at Villa Mabrouka, a 12-room hotel that was once the home of Yves Saint Laurent and Pierre Bergé, with gardens designed by Madison Cox.

Writing about Tangier in the 1950s, Truman Capote observed that “the days slide by less noticed than foam in a waterfall.” Decades later, this city on Morocco’s coast, across the Strait of Gibraltar from Spain, is significantly changed from Capote’s time, when it was an exotic retreat for café society’s demimonde, people such as Barbara Hutton and Malcolm Forbes. In recent years Tangier’s mosaic of villages—from the medina’s warren of whitewashed lanes to the Ville Moderne’s blocks of Art Deco towers to the hillside villas and gardens on Jebel Kebir—has been reinvigorated, in part by an enterprising mix of Tanjawis (the locals) and expats who continue to infuse the city with a fresh creative spirit.

Yet there is much that remains the same: the dreamy light that seduced artists from Eugène Delacroix to Henri Matisse to Mariano Fortuny, the overgrown gardens of casbah courtyards, and the cooling breezes coming off the confluence of Atlantic and Mediterranean waters. And there’s still plenty of the languorous lifestyle that captivated Capote.

“Tangier is all about settling in,” says Charlotte Claydon, whose whitewashed Moroccan Art Deco house has been in her family for more than a century. Known as Dar Sinclair, the residence sits in a grove of papyrus and lilies between thick palms and weeping willows. It’s one of numerous historic villas and townhouses renovated by international designers and other creatives who live here that can be rented. There’s arguably no better way to ease into the rhythms of daily life in Tangier. Pin Affleck, who arranges travel and accommodations through the Tangier Bureau, says that “going into a private house, you get the sense of being inside Tangier.”

When Claydon and her sister Jacquetta Wheeler began renting out Dar Sinclair, they received messages from guests worried about filling up a week. “We spent hours coming up with lists of activities, knowing full well they might only get to one or two,” Claydon says, noting that most guests make their own discoveries and end up on unplanned diversions.

Among the sisters’ can’t-miss activities: tea at Café de Paris, Sunday service at historic St. Andrew’s Church, and a camel ride, especially with kids, at Achakar Beach. A typical morning in Tangier might begin with breakfast under a banyan tree at El Morocco Club. Around 11 a.m. the markets buzz to life, including the legendary fish market in the Grand Socco, the Fondouk Chejra (weavers’ market), the antiques mall on Rue de Hollande, and the Casabarata flea market, where a sharp eye is required to spot the gems amid the seedy furniture and cheap fabrics.

Lunch in town at Alma Kitchen or El Dorado is a must for crispy falafel and plates of lamb chops. Later, as the sun sinks into the sea, it’s hard to beat the views from the roof of Villa Mabrouka, the onetime home of Yves Saint Laurent and Pierre Bergé that Jasper Conran transformed into hotel with gardens by Tangier resident Madison Cox. After a martini, the hotel’s L’Orangerie restaurant is a smart spot for a dinner of thistle tagine and grilled sole; down the street, the atmospheric Hamadi serves standout harissa soup and sizzling skewers.

Claydon and Wheeler, for their part, also advise guests to reserve time for relaxing and being transported at home, one of the great pleasures of staying in a design-centric private residence. Other places available to rent include the red-and-white-striped riad owned by decorator Gavin Houghton and photographer Boz Gagovski (who took most of the photos in this story), as well as antiques dealer Gordon Watson’s large ochre house. Both weave together Moorish and European details, with objects from Tangier and around the world that reflect the city’s cosmopolitan history. As Hamish Bowles noted in his foreword to the book Inside Tangier, “The magic of this place was that everyone who had chosen to make a home here brought their own fantasy with them.”

That book was produced by my husband, designer Nicolò Castellini Baldissera, and our home here, a 1929 townhouse named Casa Tosca, is filled with a worldly mix of objects and furnishings that we designed. Outside, our terraced garden was overseen by Umberto Pasti, whose own Tangier residence is a collection of 1930s bungalows set within meandering gardens of datura trees, rambling roses, and alocasia. For us, so much of the pleasure of this place is being at home and having people over. Creating the setting with local craftspeople and market finds becomes a pastime in itself.

It’s the same for Marco Scarani and Jamie Creel, whose house, Dar Zero, has had an illustrious lineage of owners who have contributed to its rich layers. The home is in the casbah, with a bushy fig tree and bougainvillea spilling from its courtyard over the front wall. Inside, iron klismos chairs from when designers Yves Vidal and Charles Sevigny lived there sit on well-worn bejmat and zellige tiles. There are also pieces of their own creation, such as brass bird-foot candlesticks they make in Tangier and sell in their New York shop, Creel & Gow. “There is a lot of transmission from one generation to another,” Scarani says. “Friends here inherit objects and even homes. We live in houses that have the spirits of the people who came before.”

For designers Frank de Biasi and Gene Meyer, it was the relationship with artisans that prompted them a few years ago to open Habibi Burton, their boutique where they offer pillows, painted tables, and a rotation of decorative objects, all sourced locally. “Making things for the shop gives me more freedom than making things for clients,” says de Biasi. “Because we aren’t designing to a deadline, I can have four artisans contribute to a single piece.” He also recommends Las Chicas for fashion, Kasbah Collective for regional crafts, and the bookshop Les Insolites.

Down the street, artist and curator Hicham Bouzid oversees Kiosk, a cultural salon and shop that hosts talks and exhibitions bringing together creatives from Africa, the Arab world, and Europe. It has a selection of books and artist prints, as well as a restaurant and live music. Bouzid is one of the native Tanjawis who are animating the city’s cultural life. Another is Yto Barrada, perhaps Morocco’s best-known contemporary artist, who founded the popular Cinéma Rif, where screenings of contemporary and classic films share the program with talks and other cultural events.

Across town Clos Nadir, the house shared by designer Jean-Louis Deniot and William Holloway, exists in a different world, perched among pines and looking out across the strait. Focusing on spaces for entertaining, Deniot placed a 30-foot dining table on a roof, while his living room is outfitted with a large fireplace, plush carpets, and a welcoming mix of furniture, mostly made locally. “Tangier is a land of artistic opportunity, a place for aesthetes, where stylistic adventures, contemplation, and the freedom to create are central to daily life,” says the ELLE Decor A-List designer, who is opening an office here.

Tangier’s close-knit community of creatives comes alive during the summer, which is when Veere Greeney, another ELLE Decor A-List designer, hosts an annual theatrical performance in the gardens of his mountaintop villa, Gazebo. Producer Rob Ashford assembles actors from Broadway and the West End (last summer it was Dames at Sea, featuring Darren Criss and Julianne Hough), and the shows are open to the public, though tickets are limited. It’s a full sensory experience colored by the scent of jasmine and gardenias, one that captures the eccentric artistic allure of Tangier.

The energy and interest in the city continue to grow. Deniot is creating a hotel with theater producer Jordan Roth, the Four Seasons recently announced a new property on Malabata Bay, and King Mohammed VI is behind a luxurious renovation of the historic Hotel Continental.

And while there is excitement around these new projects, what appeals most about Tangier is something older. Over lunch on Grenney’s terrace, looking out at Spain, we discussed the city’s past lives. “What makes Tangier unique is its varied history and strategic position,” he observed. “The strait made it a city of diplomacy, openness, and inclusiveness. That is its bedrock.”

This story originally appeared in the November 2026 issue of ELLE Decor.

#6797. Style Starts With A Flawless Foundation

No more bad bras! Vanity Fair Lingerie’s newest underwire bras are here to save you from back bulges, floppy straps, and other common booby traps.

A bad bra definitely ruins an outfit—and possibly even your whole day. This might sound like an overly dramatic hot take, but any woman (and by that we mean every woman) who has ever had to corral her cleavage with unflattering undergarments will tell you: the struggle is real.

We’ve all been there. Maybe you found mint-condition, underpriced, vintage mini skirt at the local thrift, you’ve been eyeing that bodycon dress, or you bought a new suit that screams lady boss in time for a dream job interview. But then you can’t find a bra that works with the wardrobe so you settle for one that gets close. And when you pair that perfect ensemble with that imperfect undergarment, it throws you off your game. You see every lump, bump and misshapen cup—and you believe everyone else sees them too. You feel every quarter of an inch that the band rides up and the girls slide down. You fuss with painful straps and migrating cups. All of that will crush the confidence that the new clothes emboldened.

With the launch of the new Flawlessly Smooth bra, Vanity Fair Lingerie promises it doesn’t have to be that way. Below, discover four ways these versatile full-coverage bras support you as the best base layer in the biz by bringing sexy to your back, Flawless Flex to your front, and supportive straps to your shoulders.

Vanity Fair Lingerie has got your back with these smooth operators. Created with Beauty Back four-way stretch technology, these bras take on every petulant protrusion and smooth over unwanted bumps so clothes can lie flat or flow uninterrupted across your back. Cups are lightly lined but have enough padding for the modest to maintain a smooth, elegant look.

Whether you’re a card-carrying member of the itty-bitty titty committee or are a classical Venus type, slight cup size fluctuations are totally normal—especially just before or during your period or in perimenopause. To combat that bit of biology, Vanity Fair Lingerie engineered built-in flex panels under the bust in the cups that shrink and expand as needed. They’re barely noticeable but hugely helpful when cups runneth over.

Closet square footage, number of carats, the amount of protein you ate today—sometimes size does matter. That’s certainly true when it comes to a bra. Flawlessly Smooth underwire bras buddy up to bosoms in myriad ways, starting with the aforementioned unique cup design. Then there’s the fact that you can adjust the band width with four hook-and-eye settings. (Most bras have only three.) Plus, straps are fully adjustable and, more ingeniously, they were made to sit closer together atop your shoulders so they stay in place and don’t bite into the skin.

#6795. When Will the Next James Bond Movie Be Out? What to Know Following the Robert Pattinson Announcement

Now we know who 007 is, when can we expect to see him in action?

Robert Pattinson has officially been announced as the new 007, and now we’re left wondering: Just when will we see him on our screens as the famous secret agent in movie history?

The 40-year-old actor was confirmed earlier this afternoon to be playing the role of James Bond by Amazon MGM Studios. He said of taking on the famous spy role, “James Bond is one of the most legendary characters in cinema, and to be entrusted with 007 is the honor of a lifetime. I’m deeply grateful to Denis, Amy, David, Tanya, and everyone at Amazon MGM Studios for their belief in me. To say this role is a dream come true is an understatement. I couldn’t be more excited about this partnership and the work ahead with this fantastic team.”

Rob takes on the role following Daniel Craig’s departure as the spy in the most recent Bond movie No Time to Die in 2021.

Given it’s already been five years since we last had a James Bond movie, just how long will we have to wait for the next one? As of now, we don’t have an official release date for the new James Bond movie. However, given it’s November 2026 and Rob’s role has only just been announced, it seems unlikely we can expect the movie to be released next year.

According to an April 2026 report from The Sun, it’s unlikely that a new movie would be coming out until at least 2028, due to director Denis Villeneuve wanting to take a break after the Dune franchise comes to an end. Dune: Part Three, which also stars Rob, comes out on December 18, 2026.

A source told the publication, “Bond won’t be back until 2028. The production mindset’s gone from ‘Let’s hurry this through’ to ‘Make sure we get this right.’ Denis wanted a long break after Dune 3 and has some broad ideas on the script. Steven will get on with drafts, before they map out the final version. That’ll take at least the whole summer.”

They added, “Realistically, if they can make January 2027 work for pre-production, then that will be a success.”

As for when we can expect the movie to come out in 2028, Digital Spy reports there are two dates booked out in Amazon’s release calendar—April 21 and August 4—for an “Untitled Amazon MGM Event Film.”

In 2022, Amazon acquired MGM Studios, and in 2025, they reached an agreement for a new joint venture with Barbara Broccoli and Michael G. Wilson, who controlled the creative rights to Bond. This new venture meant that “Amazon MGM Studios will gain creative control of the James Bond franchise following closing of the transaction.”

And since February 2025, the hunt has been on for the next leading actor to take on the role of James Bond. Also reportedly said to be in the running for the role of Bond were Callum Turner, Jack Lowden, and Josh O’Connor, among (many) others.

#6785. Robert Pattinson Is Our New James Bond—And Seemingly the Only Leading Man Left in Hollywood

He’s Batman, he’s Bond, and he’s seemingly in every other film these days. But has Hollywood’s obsession with Robert Pattinson exposed a bigger problem?

Having already dominated our cinema screens in 2026 with The Drama, The Odyssey, and Primetime, and with Dune: Part Three still to come, Robert Pattinson has somehow found room in his diary to take on another rather significant commitment: James Bond.

Yes, the man currently playing Batman is also our new 007. Somewhere, a casting director’s Rolodex is gathering dust.

At this point, I’m half expecting the British actor to pop up in a director’s cut of The Devil Wears Prada 2 wearing a cerulean jumper. Then again, he did once profess his love of taking jobs from younger actors. Clearly, he wasn’t joking.

Now, don’t get me wrong, I’m hardly immune to Rob’s charms. As someone who lived through the cultural juggernaut that was Twilight, watching his transformation from sparkly vampire to one of the most charismatic actors of his generation has been a sight to behold. But his latest appointment begs the question: Is there only one leading man left in Hollywood?

For months leading up to the announcement, reports suggested the race to become Bond had been narrowed down to Damson Idris and Aaron Taylor-Johnson. Then Callum Turner’s name entered the mix, alongside Jack Lowden, Jacob Elordi, and Paul Mescal, followed by Nicholas Hoult, Josh O’Connor, and, at the eleventh hour, Robert himself. At one point, I was half-tempted to put myself forward for the role.

Yet, in hindsight, Rob’s casting perhaps shouldn’t come as much of a surprise. Bond will mark his second collaboration with director Denis Villeneuve before their first, Dune: Part Three, has even reached theaters. Christopher Nolan has already cast him twice (in Tenet and The Odyssey) while Matt Reeves is reuniting with the actor for The Batman Part II.

For all the talk of Hollywood’s movie star drought, its most influential filmmakers seem to have found their guy. And they’re not particularly keen on letting him go. Commercially, you can understand the appeal. The Odyssey alone has amassed more than $1.8 billion at the global box office, making it the second-highest-grossing film of 2026 so far behind Spider-Man: Brand New Day.

Of course, those ticket sales aren’t solely down to Robert Pattinson. Christopher Nolan’s name and the incredible ensemble cast of Zendaya, Tom Holland, Matt Damon, and Anne Hathaway, among others, carry considerable weight, but Rob’s ability to move between intimate independent films like The Lighthouse and sprawling blockbusters makes him an increasingly valuable commodity.

According to Box Office Watch, the five films Rob has appeared in over the past two years have collectively grossed more than $2.1 billion worldwide. And that’s before he sets foot on Arrakis to face Paul Atreides and the Fremen in December.

However, what’s interesting about the British actor becoming Hollywood’s ultimate leading man is that he’s spent much of his career actively dismantling that very archetype.

Look at his recent filmography: a fiancé whose behavior in The Drama triggers an intervention of sorts; the rather unpleasant Antinous in The Odyssey; a morally complicated exploration of To Catch a Predator’s Chris Hansen in Primetime; and the hapless Jackson grappling to support his partner in the throes of postpartum depression in Die My Love. Even his Batman is more emotionally unavailable sad boy than conventional superhero.

Speaking to The Guardian in July, veteran film journalist Steven Gaydos described Robert as “an accomplished and fearless actor with tremendous range,” praising his refusal to simply give audiences what they expect.

Perhaps that’s precisely what makes him so appealing to filmmakers. But there’s a difference between recognizing an actor’s talent and an industry becoming overly reliant on it.

Historically, Bond has been a vehicle for turning actors into international superstars. Rob’s casting arguably reverses that relationship. For Amazon MGM Studios, entrusting its first Bond outing to an established star with a multigenerational fan base feels like a particularly safe bet.

But where does that leave the next generation of leading men? How do we discover another Robert Pattinson if Hollywood keeps casting Robert Pattinson?

Perhaps Hollywood doesn’t have a leading man problem so much as an imagination problem. And perhaps the next great movie star is already out there, waiting for someone to take the same chance on them.

After all, even Robert Pattinson had to be discovered once—no thanks to Jack Whitehall’s dad.

#6778. Kate Middleton and Prince William Begin Their First Overseas Tour in Four Years

The couple returns to India for the Earthshot Prize, which awards £1 million each to five environmental initiatives.

Kate Middleton and Prince William have arrived in India for a three-day official visit, their first joint overseas tour since 2022. Announced by Kensington Palace over the weekend, the November 16–18 trip is in honor of the sixth annual Earthshot Prize awards in Mumbai. It also marks a significant step in the Princess of Wales’s return to public duties following her 2024 cancer diagnosis.

The couple will attend the awards ceremony on Tuesday, November 17, when five environmental initiatives will each receive £1 million to expand their work. Founded by Prince William in 2020, the prize recognizes solutions across five areas, including restoring nature, reducing waste, and addressing climate change. Mumbai is the awards’ first Indian host city, following past editions in London, Boston, Singapore, Cape Town, and Rio de Janeiro.

The royal itinerary extends beyond the ceremony. Kensington Palace said the visit would highlight sustainable urban transportation, responsible manufacturing, youth-led environmental projects, and India’s creative industries. Today, the couple joined cricket great Sachin Tendulkar and local children for a game of street cricket in Ballard Estate, a historic neighborhood in South Mumbai. The Prince and Princess of Wales are also scheduled to attend the inaugural Children’s Earthshot Awards, recognizing environmental solutions developed by young people ages 9 to 17.

The trip is Catherine’s first overseas tour with William since she disclosed her cancer diagnosis in 2024, and her first appearance at an Earthshot ceremony since the Boston awards in 2022. It is also the couple’s second official visit to India, a decade after their April 2016 tour.

#6775. From Harry Styles to Audrey Hobert, These Major Artists Are Noticeably Missing From the 2027 Grammy Nominations

It’s that time of year again. Just as festival season comes to a close, awards season is already off to the races. While musicians and artists do it for the love of the game, you have to admit: getting a shiny trophy and industry-wide recognition from your peers is kinda nice.

While this year’s Grammy nominations included a ton of pleasant surprises (ahem, RAYE’s 11 nominations, Remi Wolf’s Best New Artist nod, and some posthumous recognition for the late Dolly Parton), they seemed to fall flat in a few areas. I’ll say it: Overall, I’m not that pressed over the 2027 nominations and am excited to see acts like Tinashe and underscores finally get recognition from the Recording Academy.

But known Grammy darlings like Harry Styles and Doja Cat were seriously snubbed this year, along with rising acts like Audrey Hobert and Malcolm Todd. So, without further ado, and in true Cosmo tradition, here are the biggest snubs from the 2027 Grammy nominations.

After seeing Audrey Hobert transform from behind-the-scenes collaborator with her (equally snubbed!) bestie, Gracie Abrams, into a front-line singer-songwriter, I thought she’d be a shoo-in for Best New Artist this year. I was an early adopter when I proclaimed “Sue Me” as the song of summer 2025, and her debut album as one of the best albums of the year. Since then, she’s been the shooting star pop music has been craving with diary-entry songwriting and intimate sleepover vibes on stage—though she’s performed to thousands at Lollapalooza and beyond.

Speaking of Audrey, her younger brother and fellow musician, Malcolm Todd, was also expected to potentially earn recognition in the Best New Artist category. Not only did he drop his third album Do That Again in June, but he also had several of his previous hits like “Earrings” and “Obsessica” take off on TikTok. Not to mention, Best New Artist nominee Don Toliver featured a sample of Malcolm’s track, “Chest Pain (I Love),” on his hit, “E85.” At least he landed that subtle nod!

My man Harry is a known Grammy darling. He’s swept in the past, but this time around, he’s been seriously snubbed. For his fourth album, Kiss All the Time. Disco, Occasionally to not be recognized at all—let alone in any of the major categories—feels strange. Some will argue that it “didn’t hit the same” as his other albums, but after taking some time off and finding his way back to the club, he dropped an incredible body of work in KISSCO. Our intro to the record is his five-minute lead single “Aperture,” which reminded us all that the revolution might take place on the dancefloor.

Sienna Spiro rightfully earned a nod in the Best New Artist category after winning the same award at this year’s VMAs. If she has a similar pipeline to Chappell Roan, she might just take home the golden gramophone, too. Her breakout moment comes off the success of her mega-viral ballad, “Die on This Hill.” And because ROTY honors production, the stunning string arrangements and her heart-wrenching vocal performance deserved some recognition here.

Another snub I’m shocked by is Noah Kahan. I personally don’t go here, but I know so many industry insiders and personal friends who ride hard for Noah, and I think they’re right to do so. His songwriting chops have made him one of the most successful artists of the streaming age, and to see his massive 2026 track “The Great Divide” get paid dust in the core categories feels out of character for the Recording Academy. (Though, his album of the same name earned a nod in the Best Contemporary Folk Album category.)

As much as I love Doja Cat, I’m not entirely surprised by her snub here. “Jealous Type,” as well as her Jack Antonoff-produced album, Vie, was a campy ode to the ’80s and should have been a major contender here. It’s unclear whether she submitted anything for consideration this year, but I find it hard to believe given Jack’s involvement and her track record as a Grammy darling. This era deserved more, but it’s not entirely a loss—I can see it having a bit of a resurgence in a few years.

Say it with me: JUSTICE FOR WEST END GIRL! Though Lily Allen and her art directors, Leith Clark and Nieves González, rightfully earned a nod in the Best Album Cover category, she didn’t land any in the core four categories. She made a powder-blue polka-dot puffer coat instantly recognizable, and along with a Duane Reade bag, it’ll serve as “gay Halloween” costume inspo for decades to come.

But back to the music—Lily hadn’t released an album since 2018 before she poured her heart out on West End Girl. It’s a deeply confessional record about her personal life and the downfall of her public marriage with Stranger Things star David Harbour, but it was also a comeback for the ages. To lay everything out as she did takes serious guts, and she did it the only way she knows: with her signature wit and deliciously unexpected production choices, standing ten toes down. (While…maybe wearing a dress printed with actual receipts and text messages, or a bib that reads “CUCK” in all caps.) Lily, I never doubted you!

#6774. I Walked After Every Meal for Two Months, and My Digestion and Energy Levels Have Never Been Better

Experts weigh in on why sitting is the worst thing you can do after eating

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

It’s almost 9:00 p.m., and my work dinner in Manhattan’s SoHo neighborhood is finally coming to a close. As the people around me start to get up and call their Ubers, I head out the door to go home—on foot. After a lavish dinner, most people’s last thought is to get a brisk walk in, but for me, it’s crucial—not to hit a step count or burn calories, but as my version of a digestif, if you will.

My idea to walk after dinner came from a video I saw months ago from a TikTok nutritionist who touted a brisk 10-minute walk after meals as the key to boosting metabolism, regulating blood sugar levels, and helping your stomach digest food. My two favorite pastimes are eating and walking. I never thought the two would go hand in hand.

“Sitting briefly after eating isn’t inherently harmful, but prolonged, uninterrupted sitting can lead to a higher post-meal blood-sugar response compared with breaking it up with short walks,” says Elizabeth Sharp Edens, an internal medicine and obesity medicine physician, functional medicine practitioner, and founder of HMW Method. A 2022 meta-analysis in Sports Medicine found that light-intensity walking reduced post-meal glucose by almost 17 percent. The same study found that standing breaks after meals reduced glucose spikes by 9.5 percent on average. The TL;DR: Research says that any type of movement can improve glucose levels.

We know walking can do wonders—it can clear the mind, help you lean out, and even boost dopamine—but why is it a solution for feeling less full and avoiding afternoon crashes? “When you walk, your muscles use glucose for energy,” Edens explains. “Muscle contractions move glucose transporters called GLUT4 to the surface of muscle cells, helping draw glucose out of the bloodstream through a pathway that doesn’t depend on insulin.” Since your legs, the vehicle for walking, contain some of the biggest muscles in your body, it makes sense that walking effectively stimulates muscle contractions that move glucose transporters. “Walking breaks are a simple way to support blood sugar management while getting your steps in,” she adds.

While many rely on peptides, green powders, and “magic” supplements, walking is an easy, mindless (and free) way to improve digestion and prevent blood-sugar crashes. “Even two to five minutes after a meal can help, ideally before the glucose peak, typically 60 to 90 minutes after eating,” Kiran Krishnan, a research microbiologist, the founder and chief science officer of the Biome Learning Center, and Dose key opinion leader, adds.

I often joke after large meals that I will have to be rolled home. The good news: Walking can also reduce the overwhelming feeling of fullness. “Walking also helps the gut physically,” Krishnan says, citing a study that found that a slow walk after a meal significantly shortened the time it took the stomach to empty half its contents. “For people prone to bloating, mild [walking] cut intestinal gas retention from 45 percent to 24 percent and eased symptoms,” he adds. Plus, Krishnan says studies show reduced LDL cholesterol levels and improved liver function in those who walked after meals.

Then there’s good old gravity, says Caitlin Hosmer, a Maine-based registered dietitian and co-founder of MenoPantry. “Gravity helps too, which is part of why walking may help people who feel uncomfortably full or get reflux after meals,” she explains. “Gentle movement and staying upright help food move out of the stomach and along the digestive tract.”

Ten minutes isn’t long, but studies show it’s just as effective as a longer walk. A 2025 study published by PubMed Central found that a 10-minute walk lowered glucose measurements by 6 percent and a 30-minute walk lowered glucose levels by 5 percent, meaning just 10 minutes of walking is all you need to help reduce a post-meal glucose spike. If you can walk for 30 minutes, an hour, or even longer—that’s great. But just 10 minutes can work just as well.

What matters most is how quickly you get moving. “Glucose typically peaks 60 to 90 minutes after eating, and moving before that peak is what blunts the spike,” Hosmer says. “A walk puts your muscles to work just as glucose from the meal is flooding into the bloodstream.” At first, I questioned whether I had to walk after every meal. “Put your effort where it counts most: after your largest, most carbohydrate-rich meal,” Hosmer advises, adding that for most, that’s after dinner, but walking after all three meals is ideal.

My post-meal walks varied—some days I would walk for 30 minutes or even an hour, depending on how much time I had and where I was going next. But as I started incorporating them into my work schedule, especially on busy office days, I often had only 10 minutes to spare. When in a pinch, I would get up and walk around the office floor and, at home, do air squats, which engage the same large leg muscles as walking and can be just as effective as a post-meal walk.

It may feel strange to pencil in time to walk after every meal, but the benefits I have noticed surpass any inconvenience or awkwardness. I have fewer crashes after I eat, whereas I previously would feel lethargic after every meal. I’ve also noticed improved digestion. While this can be chalked up to a number of things (my beloved vibration plate, greens powders, or digestive enzymes), I always feel lighter and less full after a post-meal walk.

It’s also not meant to feel daunting or like a chore. “Your post-dinner walk isn’t a workout. It’s a way of helping your body handle the meal,” Hosmer says. “The one real ‘wrong way’ is skipping it because you think it has to be long or perfect. I often say something is better than nothing, and that’s especially true here. Even a few minutes of walking, started soon after you finish eating, can blunt the blood sugar rise, and it may help your digestion too.”

#6771. How ‘Rivals’ Created an Age-Gap Relationship We’re Actually Rooting For

R ivals has returned to our screens, and Rutshire’s residents are hornier than ever. But Rutag fans might have to hang on a little longer to see them enter their relationship era. Frustrating? Not necessarily, as maybe this intense slow-burn is exactly the reason Rivals has managed to create an age-gap relationship we’re actually rooting for.

There is a lot of awareness around age-gap couples in pop culture right now, and audiences seem very done with the classic younger woman, older man dynamic. Movies like A24’s Babygirl and Amazon’s The Idea of You have gone some way toward challenging outdated tropes around age-related power dynamics, but that still doesn’t erase how Hollywood handled things in the past (Pretty Little Liars and Fifty Shades of Grey come to mind).

Rivals is a bit of an anomaly in how it has tackled Rupert and Taggie’s age-gap relationship so far, and the slower pace seems to have played a very necessary part in that. Rutag’s slow-burn underpins the entire foundation of their connection. What’s working for me personally is that Rupert hasn’t been rushing Taggie emotionally or physically to match where he’s at in his current stage of life. In fact, even though he seems besotted with her—she’s sort of become his moral touchstone—he’s doing his best to stay away from her. He seems to find the idea of letting her down (or fully letting her in) absolutely terrifying, despite how much he clearly cares.

Rivals gains further grace from viewers for its willingness to openly acknowledge Rupert and Taggie’s age gap as being an obstacle to overcome in their relationship, rather than just glossing over it or choosing to pretend it doesn’t exist. Rupert even acknowledges the age gap as being an issue himself by making a promise to Declan in season 1 that he’ll stay away from Taggie. By showing us these hurdles early on, it gives deeper insight into what a relationship would eventually mean for these two and what they’d be up against.

Watching Taggie gain life experience and have her own adventures of self-discovery—not everything has to involve Rupert—has helped them remain exciting to us as a potential couple. Her catering business seems to be thriving, for example. The slow-burn gives Taggie the opportunity to forge her own identity and ambitions, which would be important to have going into a relationship with Rupert, given their differences in life experience. Taggie is slowly blossoming into herself, without falling into that cliché trap of trying to change who she is to impress a man, and that’s empowering and necessary.

As long as Rivals continues to explore the age gap with care and consideration, I think fans will keep rooting for Rutag’s romance, no matter how long the slow-burn takes.

#6769. Is Belrose University From ‘Campus Drivers’ Real? Let’s Break Down the Dreamy Filming Locations

Living vicariously through the characters in a romance show is always fun, but when the love story unfolds in a gorgeous location, it makes the fantasy even better. Are you enjoying the French series Campus Drivers on Prime Video, and feeling the itch to start exploring the area on Google Maps, Airbnb, and even Zillow? These Campus Drivers filming locations will get you started.

The series follows a college student named Lois, who becomes temporarily homeless after getting dumped by her boyfriend/roommate and moves in with her neighbor. He happens to have a ride-share app, which is definitely not just for transporting drunk coeds he and his friends want to hook up with, and is named Lane. Normal meet-cute stuff. Also, Lois and Lane... get it?!

But the setting really stands out. Dare I say it, the hallowed hall and shiny shores of Campus Drivers is far more glamorous than anything you’d see on Off Campus, Gossip Girl, and even Elite. The beaches, clubs, and architecture are beyond what we’re used to on collegiate shows like this. Here’s how they brought it to life.

Nope! The Prime Video series takes place at a fictional school on the French Riviera. However, according to Decider, Quill attended Carlone University (locally referred to as Campus Carlone) in Nice. So if you’re looking to study abroad, that might be a good place to start.

This is a major change from the book series, however, which takes place in the United States at a fictional school called Sycamore University. But since Quill herself is French, and went to school in France, this doesn’t feel like that big of a change.

The Prime Video series filmed on the French Riviera, a.k.a. the Côte d’Azur, in the South of France between Nice, Cannes, and Antibes. Lane’s apartment is filmed at the Marina Baie des Anges, a famous waterfront complex in Villeneuve-Loubet. And finally, Villa Eilenroc in Cap d’Antibes is where they filmed Belrose University.

While we can’t promise that your ride-share driver will become a fling (or more), these are fairly easily attainable vacation spots. Beaches like Les Ondes and Port Mallet on the Cap d’Antibes appear on the series and are open to the public. Start your planning now!

#6767. The Best Outfit Secret Is the One You Don’t See

Fashion editor and stylist Indya Brown on why the new Vanity Fair Lingerie’s Flawlessly Smooth Underwire Bra deserves a spot in your everyday rotation.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

“The most important part of your outfit is the one that no one sees, because that is where the look really begins,” says Indya Brown. As a fashion editor and stylist, she knows the little details make a big difference: the right fit, the right silhouette, and perhaps most important, a base layer that lets the rest of your outfit do its thing.

That’s the idea behind the latest Vanity Fair Lingerie Flawlessly Smooth Underwire Bra. It’s the kind of behind-the-scenes essential that makes getting dressed feel easier. Think of it as the quiet MVP in your top drawer: supportive, polished, and ready to work with whatever’s on the agenda.

It starts with lightly lined cups, which offer modesty and shape for a smooth look beneath clothing; rounded with a built-in flex panel under the bust, they’re designed to accommodate size fluctuations. Four hook-and-eye adjustment positions let you fine-tune the fit, while the straps are set closely together so they stay comfortably in place throughout the day.

Then there’s the Beauty Back four-way stretch back-smoothing technology, created to smooth lumps and bumps beneath clothes. The result is a foundation that feels supportive without stealing the spotlight. For Brown, that’s more than just a practical first step. “When you have a great foundation, it really changes how you show up,” she says. It’s the starting point for great style: helping a sharp blazer sit smoothly, a favorite dress falling just right, an everyday tee feeling a little more pulled together.

Choose the color that works best with your wardrobe, then let this everyday essential quietly do its job. Because when your bra is comfortable and your silhouette feels just right, you can focus on the outfit—and wherever the day takes you.

#6763. Dakota Johnson’s Latest Dose of Pajama Dressing Is All About Mismatched Prints

Whether hitting the red carpet in a little lingerie-inspired slip or dressing up a long ivory robe for an event, Dakota Johnson has long loved a good pajama dressing moment. And on Thursday night, she gave her go-to style another spin—this time with a breezy two-piece set that could seriously take her from a night out to a night in.

Courtesy of Italian label Loretta Caponi, the ensemble was composed of a long-sleeve midi dress and relaxed trousers, each crafted from black satin, adorned with delicate gold floral embroidery, and trimmed with scalloped lace. The loose dress, with its choker collar, deep V neckline and drop shoulders, was secured in the front by a line of buttons—which Johnson left undone, wearing the piece as a robe. The coordinating trousers were defined by a pleated waist and fluid silhouette.

Underneath, the Fifty Shades of Grey actor embraced another one of her favorite trends—naked dressing—with a sheer black lace cami that was embroidered with a floral design of its own. And, for a third pop of pattern, she grabbed a small leopard-print Valentino bag.

Johnson wore the look to Thursday night’s Los Angeles screening of Flesh Impact, Maggie Gyllenhaal’s forthcoming short film that imagines what Marilyn Monroe’s life might have been like had she lived to be 100 years old. For the project, Johnson transformed into Monroe at the height of her fame, blonde hair and all. It premiered at Venice Film Festival in September, though a theatrical or streaming release date has not yet been announced.

#6762. The Men on 'The Gilded Age' Don't Deserve Your Praise

They may be gentlemen, but their chivalry is sorely lacking.

It feels like television shows (and real life, let’s face it) are currently overrun with men who constantly fall short when it comes to matters of the heart. In the latest season of Nobody Wants This, many fans noticed how Joanne was constantly making sacrifices for their relationship while Noah just… wasn’t. The latest season of Love Is Blind also found the guys seriously wanting, with many failing to put in a bare minimum of effort to make a love connection. And it saddens me to say that this trend of low-effort men seems to have made its way to the fourth season of The Gilded Age as well. That’s right, my friends—not even the 19th century is safe from this epidemic of male apathy.

We may only be a few episodes into the season, yet already viewers have witnessed several examples where the men in the show are found lacking when it comes to their romantic partnerships. First, there’s Larry, who appears to have zero appreciation for the tough position Marian is now in after they spent the night together. Although he does suggest they get married, Marian worries it’s only out of obligation, and he seems to not really grasp the severity of the situation she’s in, that social ruin could be waiting for her if anyone found out about their night together.

Many fans online have been quick to come to Larry’s defense, criticizing Marian for self-sabotaging or playing the part of a martyr. But I disagree. Her concerns are valid and Larry is being ambiguous about his feelings. Now would be the perfect time for a grand romantic gesture on Larry’s part to show Marian how serious he is about their relationship. Where’s the big, swoonworthy “You have bewitched me body and soul” moment? Where is the standing-outside-her-window-with-a-boombox (or 1800s equivalent) scene? I want to root for them, but Larry is making it extremely difficult with his underwhelming devotion.

Then there’s Hector, who was praised by Bertha for choosing to save Gladys’s life over their unborn child’s during an extremely difficult labor. While it is nice to know how much affection he has for his wife, it never should’ve been his choice to begin with, so applauding him for “saving” Gladys feels like a very low bar to set for these male characters. I understand this was a customary practice at the time and him making the decision was more the doctor’s doing than anything else, but now he’s also gatekeeping that she can’t have any more children. Watching a man determine when a woman should know something about her own body feels outrageous in any time period. I’m not saying that Hector is a bad guy, but I don’t think he deserves so much admiration for doing the bare minimum.

Meanwhile, George Russell continues to deal with (or rather, not deal with) his ongoing laudanum addiction, which is already growing tiresome with each passing episode. His relationship with Bertha has never been worse. She is putting in all the effort to repair their marriage while he remains cold and distant, lying to her at every turn. I understand this change in his behavior is due to his drug dependency, but should that make all of his missteps completely forgivable? Bertha assured him at the end of Sunday night’s episode, “I won’t disappoint you.” But it is he who is the disappointment right now.

Unfortunately, not even Dr. William Kirkland can get a free pass, despite his impeccable behavior leading up to his wedding day. Buying Peggy a house, standing up to his mother, and refusing to let her dictate the wedding — those are all major green flags. Yet next week’s promo indicated William might pursue a new job in Washington, which feels completely out of nowhere considering he just bought a house for the two of them. Also, this is something he should probably consult his wife about before making any public declarations. Is the honeymoon phase over already?

Bottom line: Chivalry may not be completely dead on this show, but its pulse is weak at best and if these gentlemen don’t do something about it soon we’re going to have even bigger problems than prematurely pouring milk into someone else’s tea.

#6760. Sam Hartman’s Exes Have Entered the Chat After He Was Announced as the Next ‘Bachelor’ Lead

One likened his personality to a vegetable…

Surprise! Sam Hartman has officially gone from being the Washington Commanders’ quarterback to becoming the lead for The Bachelor season 30. Some fans are all too familiar with the NFL athlete, but in case you don’t go here in the sports department…there’s apparently already some drama behind the scenes.

After ABC officially announced Sam as the next Bachelor on Friday, November 13, some of his ex-girlfriends reacted to the news. Let’s just say they didn’t seem too pleased with his quarterback-to-reality-star pipeline.

An interior designer from Naples named Becca Hunt shared an old photo of herself with Sam—who she claims to have dated in the past—on TikTok.

“The most toxic relationship of my life is now the bachelor LOL,” her post reads.

In a separate comment, she added, “look yall i am not going to say anything more abt this i just thought it was ironic because it’s a dating show.”

Naturally, her comments section went wild over the revelation.

“This tracks. A man can’t have a face card this lethal and be pure of heart,” one fan alleged, while another chimed in, “I said ‘wow he’s hot’ then they asked what he was looking for in a wife and he said ‘competitiveness’ and lost interest immediately.”

As it turns out, she isn’t the only ex of Sam’s who has spoken out amid his announcement.

Lauren Blake Boultier, who reportedly dated the football star for seven months (and who, earlier this year, was accused of photoshopping her face onto Black content creator Tatiana Elizabeth’s body) jokingly told TMZ that he has the “personality of asparagus.”

As for what that really means? According to Lauren, “He’s good for you, but he’s not exactly the most exciting thing on the menu.”

She emphasized that she’s “genuinely rooting for him, even if their own romance didn’t work out,” and even named some of his redeeming qualities: “family oriented, down to earth and absolutely husband material for the right woman.”

While the model and DAZN boxing host is more adventurous and spontaneous, she claimed that Sam loves his routine, which includes football, family, and fishing in his free time.

What’s more: She took to TikTok to post a photo of herself and Sam together with the caption, “When you get casted to go on the Bachelor but the Bachelor turns out to be your ex”…only to delete said post.

As far as exes go, Sam is also rumored to have dated former Wake Forest soccer star Tyla Ochoa (having confirmed he was dating a Wake Forest soccer player during a 2024 interview), but she has yet to weigh in on the casting news.

So…what is the truth? Is this man boring, toxic, both, or neither? Guess we’ll have to find out when The Bachelor season 30 hits ABC next year.

#6758. Key Takeaways From Luxury’s Q3 Earnings

China’s macro environment has been a major drag on luxury’s third-quarter earnings, causing most companies to fall short of market expectations.

Analysts expected growth to slow in Q3, but conditions in China turned out worse than anticipated. The sector’s average organic sales growth ended at 4% in Q3 2026, versus 7.8% in Q2 (calculated by Goldman Sachs on the basis of a simple average of luxury companies, excluding currency impact).

Kering stock plummeted 17%, following the announcement that growth at the company had dropped to 1% in Q3 (vs +2% in Q2), including a 6% decrease at its largest brand, Gucci. (Gucci retail sales declined by 9% on a comparable basis.) Kering and LVMH stock are each down 37% year to date, while the Hermès share price has fallen 36%.

“Everyone is staying on the sidelines,” Goldman Sachs luxury analyst Erwan Rambourg says, referring to investors’ cautious stance. While he believes that Q3 marks the trough in the luxury downturn, he thinks that investors prefer to wait for tangible signs of an improvement rather than catch a falling knife.

And after a stellar performance in the first half of the year, jewelry brands began decelerating in Q3. Sales at Richemont’s jewelry maisons, including Cartier and Van Cleef & Arpels, rose 14% versus 24% in Q2. Sales at LVMH’s watches and jewelry division and Kering’s jewelry arm, the latter of which includes Boucheron and Pomellato, were up 6% and 11%, respectively (vs 11% and 18% in Q2). Sales at other Hermès sectors, including jewelry and home, were down 1% (vs 4% in Q2).

“Richemont’s jewelry maisons growing 14% in the fiscal second quarter is a slowdown, but it’s still tremendously better than pretty much anyone else in the sector,” Rambourg flags. “That gives a first-half average of just below 20% top-line growth for jewelry, which is the core profit driver of the company. For the first time in a while, margins have increased.”

Here are the rest of the key takeaways from luxury’s Q3 earnings.

China’s slowdown deepened after three years of deceleration. Growth at LVMH decreased 5% in Asia, while Kering fell 8% and Hermès came in flat. “It should be noted that the macroeconomic environment [in China] remains unfavorable,” Hermès’s VP of finance Eric du Halgouët told analysts during the company’s earnings call on October 22.

While the broader picture in China remained largely unchanged, with continued weakness in the real estate market, two additional factors weighed on bottom lines: the decline in equity market performance, and China’s new taxation of foreign assets. The law on taxation, which was announced in late July and applies to offshore trusts, a widely used vehicle among high-net-worth Chinese individuals, had a significant impact on the sentiment of affluent consumers; the rules have retroactive effect from 2023, and the deadline to declare and pay was just 90 days.

“The two growth engines of the industry for the past 20 years are the middle-income consumer and the Chinese consumer,” says Morgan Stanley managing director Édouard Aubin. These two engines are now barely running.

Using LVMH as a proxy, the Chinese cohort was flattish in Q2 and down approximately 15% in Q3. Richemont also highlighted the challenges in the mainland China market, despite seeing a positive trend in Greater China. “ If anybody thinks we’re less affected by [market conditions in] China, on the contrary,” chairman Johann Rupert told reporters during a media call on Friday.

Some soft luxury brands are still doing well, including in China. Ralph Lauren’s Asia revenue saw 28% growth in Q3. Greater China led retail growth for Burberry (+5%), and the Greater China region was up 5.7% for Zegna Group. In the private space, Chanel and Longchamp are also understood to perform well in China.

One category doing surprisingly well in China is prestige beauty. L’Oréal Group posted 7.8% growth in North Asia in Q3. “North Asia was back to high-single-digit growth for the first time since the first half of 2022. This marks a step change in L’Oréal’s growth algorithm in our view,” HSBC analysts, including Olivier Nicolaï, wrote in a note. Puig revenues rose 12.9% in Asia-Pacific. Does it suggest that Chinese customers are trading down — buying lower-ticket items like fragrances and skincare?

And is there a brighter outlook ahead for luxury brands in China? “There aren’t many hopes for things to improve in the short term,” Rambourg says. “I think China being very tough is probably going to be the case for the next two or three quarters. Visibility is quite poor. And if you leave aside bases of comparison, there’s no key underlying reason for China to do better.”

“Q3 was a continuation of the K-shaped economy we’ve seen in recent quarters — where the top end grows, but the lower end lags as the middle-income consumer has been priced out. The brands at the top of the pyramid are doing well; the rest are not,” Aubin says.

Zegna brand (+12.7% in Q3), Brunello Cucinelli (+13.5% in Q3), Loro Piana (“enjoying very strong growth,” according to LVMH CFO Cécile Cabanis) and Chanel (up double digits in Q1, Q2 and Q3, according to industry sources) are in the former category.

At LVMH, Cabanis noted that Louis Vuitton sales came in just behind the group’s fashion division at -5%, while Dior was flat. Rambourg remains bullish on Dior. “One, Dior is more exposed to China than some other brands, so that’s painful. Two, they have a men’s business, which is not doing as well as women’s. Three, I think it takes a bit more time for [creative director] Jonathan Anderson’s products to gain weight within the brand, but it’s certainly coming,” he explains.

Louis Vuitton felt the repercussions of both its legal dispute with Chinese tea brand Molly Tea and an issue with a shipping partner — the latter weighed on quarterly growth by a little over one percentage point. Add to that a tough year-on-year comparison basis: Louis Vuitton opened a boat-shaped mega-store in Shanghai in June 2025, dubbed The Louis, which generated a surge in foot traffic. The brand is undergoing a change in strategy, according to Rambourg: “They’re in the process of moving under the influence of deputy CEO Damien Bertrand, from what Louis Vuitton was 15 months ago, which was colorful, cheerful, fashion-driven, to something more institutional, more focused on the origin of the brand, which is the art of travel.”

A Shanghai Fashion Week attendee wears a full Louis Vuitton look.

Kering’s recent creative changes were on the agenda of the group’s earnings call. Shortly before its Q3 earnings, Kering announced the departures of Anthony Vaccarello from Saint Laurent and Seán McGirr from McQueen. And on November 5 and 6, the group announced the departure of Pierpaolo Piccioli from Balenciaga, with Martine Rose to succeed him.

While still outperforming the sector, Hermès’s growth decelerated in Q3 (+4.3% in Q3 vs +6.7% in Q2). “While Hermès’s leather goods category re-accelerated to nearly 11%, some investors questioned whether the weakness in other categories, such as ready-to-wear and accessories, could foreshadow a similar slowdown in leather goods,” Barclays director Carole Madjo says. Risk of [handbag market] saturation in China and the increasingly structured secondhand market could potentially weigh on demand over time.”

After years of Miu Miu outperforming Prada, the trend reversed in Q2, with Prada (6.3%) surpassing Miu Miu (2.6%), and continued into Q3, as Prada grew 1% while Miu Miu contracted 3%, likely due to a higher exposure to China and the Middle East  relative to the average of the sector.

Rambourg also stressed the important polarization across brands: “In the soft luxury space, we consider Chanel is now the biggest brand — bigger than Louis Vuitton — and growing very quickly. And so that’s taking a bit of the air from the others, notably adjacent brands like Hermès. In hard luxury, Cartier is continuing to do very well and is also taking a bit of air away from their competitors. So in both soft and hard luxury, you have one dominant brand which counterintuitively is growing very quickly.”

The US might remain a luxury growth driver, but things began to decelerate this quarter, partly because of a tougher comparison basis. Hermès was up 8.8% in the Americas in Q3 (vs 13.7% in Q2), and Kering’s North American retail revenue rose 1% (vs 10% in Q2). “Investors are questioning whether this signals the beginning of a normalization in the US, and whether it will be confirmed in Q4 and beyond,” Barclays’ Madjo says.

Goldman Sachs’s Rambourg dismissed concerns: “I do believe that the little softness in Q3 is temporary. I foresee a re-acceleration in Q4 linked to the easier comparison basis and the reality that holiday campaigns, new products and more thoughtful pricing should motivate people to buy. Not to mention that you had a lot of wealth creation from equity markets. Luxury demand has been a bit lagging, but I think it’s bound to pick up.”

It is unclear whether the US midterm elections — which saw the Democrats take control of Congress for the remainder of Trump’s presidency — impacted the third quarter. Some customers possibly adopted a wait-and-see approach to spending ahead of the results, which could now be released and influence Q4. “Midterm elections typically have less impact than presidential elections,” Madjo notes. Asked what impact the outcome might have on Richemont’s business, CFO Burkhart Grund said: “We’re just a few days after the midterm elections and we haven’t really picked up anything meaningful.”

Europe was up 4% for LVMH, down 5% for Kering’s fashion and leather goods division (including a 9% drop for Gucci), and up 7.2% for Hermès. “It is Europeans who are spending in Europe,” said Hermès’s Eric du Halgouët. Hermès’s sales in France decreased in Q3 to 3.6% from 6.2% in Q2, as the heatwave impacted footfall in the Parisian stores.

As brands began gearing up for the crucial holiday season, protests erupted across France, Italy, Spain, Belgium and Portugal. France and Italy are significant European markets. France accounted for 8% of LVMH’s total revenue in the first nine months of 2026 and 9.6% of Hermès’s nine-month revenues. “The comments on the impact of the protests in Europe were quite vague. It’s too hard to say and hard to quantify,” Madjo says.

LVMH’s earnings call took place before the so-called Act I, the day of major urban unrest on October 17. On October 22, Hermès’s du Halgouët said it was too early to assess impact on local stores’ business and tourism. (The Hermès store in Marseille was looted and store windows in Paris, Nantes and Lyon were broken.) “It’s not just a China problem. Now, you have a French-Italian problem on top of that,” Aubin notes, referring to the possible effects on Q4.

Despite the lack of visibility in China and Europe, Goldman Sachs’s Rambourg anticipates a progression in Q4 and next year. “Because we see the US re-accelerating, and importantly, we see self-help measures — product launches and better pricing — motivating consumers to come back,” he says.
