#6714. 8 Fresh Perfumes Everyone Will love (Yes, Even Maximalists)

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

These dreamy scents smell like soap, fresh laundry, blooming greenery, and more.

Even though I love writing about fragrance, I don’t necessarily want to smell like perfume. Most of the time I wish the scent of my body wash lasted for hours or that my clothes eternally smelled like I just pulled them out of the dryer. But, as we all know, a majority of those wonderful clean scents disappear in an instant. That’s why I made it my mission to find the dreamiest fresh perfumes.

Fresh perfumes typically feature notes like ambroxan and aldehydes (two synthetic compounds that smell, you guessed it, clean and fresh), as well as citrus and musk. They can be aquatic, green, warm, or even floral, but of course, there’s an overarching subtlety about them that fits in this specific family. There's no better smell in the world (to me) than soap, fresh laundry, or simply skin, but "clean" means something different to everyone. That’s why we found an array of fresh perfumes to choose from. Just take a look at some of our faves...

If you want to smell shower-fresh at all times (who doesn’t?), keep reading to discover team Cosmo’s favorite fresh perfumes.

One Cosmo editor says: “Nothing compares to the scent of a mineralic sea breeze, and Jo Malone bottled the essence perfectly. If I close my eyes when I spritz Sea Salt & Bergamot, I’m transported back to the rocky Cornish coast. This perfume is simple in the best way possible. Even though it has just three notes—bergamot, sea salt, and driftwood—it’s completely unforgettable. I’ve gone through a full bottle since it launched this summer.”—MARY HONKUS, CONTRIBUTING BEAUTY EDITOR

One Cosmo editor says: “Alto Astral is simply fresh. (Fitting that it won the Best Fresh Perfume category in our Holy Grail Beauty Awards, right?) Don’t be afraid to layer with it though! It works with every scent family.”—JASMINE HYMAN, ASSISTANT BEAUTY EDITOR

One Cosmo editor says: "Sometimes I want to smell exactly like a bubble. And Replica has, ahem, replicated that fragrance. It's the epitome of squeaky clean. Somehow, this reminds me of being in the tub as a kid and using fluffy soap bubbles to turn myself into Santa Claus. That may not sound super appealing as a fragrance, but trust me, with the additions of creamy coconut and lavender, it's amazing."—JAYNE LEMIRE, EXECUTIVE ASSISTANT

#6713. Whole Wheat vs. Sourdough: Which Bread Is Healthier?

How to pick the loaf that fits your life.

Whole wheat bread used to be the gold standard of quick, healthy carbs. Unlike white bread, it uses flour milled from the entire wheat kernel, providing extra fiber and vitamins. But sourdough has exploded in popularity over the past decade, offering a seemingly more natural option made from just sourdough starter, flour, and water. Is this ancient breadmaking method healthier than whole wheat, even if it uses white flour?

As a dietitian, I see this showdown between whole wheat and sourdough all the time. People wonder if the rustic, tangy charm of sourdough beats the wholesome, fiber-packed power of whole wheat. Let’s slice into the details and figure out which loaf deserves a spot on your counter.

Whole wheat is exactly what it sounds like. This bread is made using flour from the entire wheat kernel—the bran, germ, and endosperm. Here’s what that means:

Because it includes all parts of the kernel, 100% whole wheat bread delivers fiber, essential vitamins like folate and niacin, and important minerals such as iron and magnesium. That fiber helps you feel satisfied after eating, supports digestion, and can support blood sugar management and heart health. This combination makes this particular bread a smart, nutrient-dense choice for everyday eating.

It’s a reliable, widely available, research-backed whole grain—and most Americans don’t eat the recommended servings of whole grains every day, which the USDA suggests is more than half of the grains we eat. So, choosing whole-grain bread can help people meet this recommendation.

It seems like everyone and their mother got into baking sourdough a few years ago. We all know it’s delicious, but what makes it special from a health perspective isn't just its ingredients—it’s how it is made.

Sourdough isn’t defined by a specific type of flour. You can make it with all-purpose, rye, or whole-wheat flour; some loaves use enriched/fortified flour for an extra boost of iron and B vitamins. And its magic lies in the fermentation process. Instead of commercial yeast, sourdough relies on a “starter,” which is a living mix of wild yeast and beneficial bacteria (specifically, lactobacilli).

This fermentation process does some pretty amazing things:

In a side-by-side battle, let's see which bread comes out on top:

If your primary goal is to maximize your fiber intake and get a consistent, nutrient-packed loaf, 100% whole wheat is an excellent choice.

If you're looking for a bread that’s easier to digest and offers enhanced mineral absorption, deliciously tangy sourdough is your go-to.

But it is possible to have the best of both worlds. Grab (or bake) a sourdough bread made with 100% whole wheat flour for fiber, nutrient density, and fermentation perks.

Whether you’re Team Sourdough or Team Whole Wheat, there’s one universal truth: your body, and let’s be honest, your soul, will thank you. Because we all know that carbs are good for the soul.

#6712. 10 Best Sunscreens for Face That You’ll Actually Enjoy Wearing

When people talk about the “good old days,” I stay quiet—I’m pretty certain I was born in the right generation. I love waiting in line for $10 artisanal matcha, and I love having access to hundreds of sunscreens that don’t pill, feel greasy, or leave behind a ghoulish white cast. Considering that sunscreen is arguably the most important step in your beauty routine (every dermatologist and aesthetician worth their license would agree), having so many options feels particularly worth celebrating, especially given how much the global sunscreen market has evolved.

If you need a refresher on why we talk about sunscreen so much at Vogue, board-certified dermatologist Marnie Nussbaum points out that while our skin-care routines may include plenty of salmon sperm and copper peptides, the most effective step is also the least exciting: sunscreen. Used diligently and consistently, it can help prevent much of the damage we might otherwise try to address later, including collagen and elastin loss, skin laxity, hyperpigmentation, fine lines, and, most importantly, skin cancer.

As for the market itself, board-certified dermatologist Lindsey Zubritsky, MD, FAAD, attributes much of the category’s growth to increased consumer education. “In the past few years, there has been so much incredible education about the importance of wearing sunscreen every day,” she says. “With more people looking to incorporate SPF into their daily routines, the market has drastically expanded.” Today, that means everything from invisible formulas and tinted sunscreens to SPF lip balms and on-the-go sticks.

Another perk of being alive in 2026? Having easier access to formulas that were once largely limited to their home countries. Whether you’re discovering Korean, Japanese, and Australian sunscreen innovations on TikTok or asking a friend to bring back a French pharmacy favorite, it’s never been easier to explore what the rest of the world has to offer. The US has historically been slower to approve newer sunscreen filters than other markets, although that’s beginning to change. In June 2026, the FDA approved bemotrizinol, a UV filter already widely used abroad and the first new sunscreen ingredient permitted in the US in more than 25 years. Still, international markets offer formulators a broader range of UV filters, helping explain the variety of textures, finishes, and formulations available overseas.

Sunscreen has come a long way from formulas that focused primarily on UVB protection. Broad-spectrum options added UVA protection, and newer formulas have made daily SPF easier to wear. Board-certified dermatologist Dara Spearman, MD, FAAD, adds that newer formulas are not only more wearable but also easier to incorporate into everyday routines. “They’re more lightweight and suitable for everyday use across skin types,” she explains. Many newer tinted formulas are designed to blend more seamlessly into a wider range of skin tones. Several also contain iron oxides, which help protect against visible light, making them especially beneficial for those with melasma or hyperpigmentation.

But knowing how important sunscreen is and actually wearing it every day are two different things. According to board-certified dermatologist Sandra Oska, MD, daily use remains inconsistent. “There has been a genuine shift in how consumers think about sunscreen, but the majority of people still aren’t wearing it every day,” she says. Men, in particular, tend to underuse SPF, while women and Gen Z are generally more consistent. Even among regular users, common mistakes like underapplying can limit protection.

Ultimately, usability is what drives consistency. As Oska notes: “What are consumers actually going to use first thing in the morning, and what’s going to make it into their bag for reapplication?” Formulas that layer well with skin care tend to win out for daily use, while sticks, powders, and SPF lip balms have become essentials for touch-ups throughout the day.

After testing dozens of sunscreens—from the classics to each new bottle that hits our desk—and speaking with esteemed dermatologists and estheticians, we evaluated them all to bring you this edit of the best of the best. (Each of them has been tested and worn IRL!) Below is a look at all the best facial sunscreens of 2026 so far.

British Vogue’s Ellie Davis tests the Beauty of Joseon Relief Sun sunscreen.

According to Dr. Nussbaum, sunscreen helps protect against both UVA and UVB rays, which cause different types of damage. UVB rays primarily cause sunburn and can damage DNA, increasing the risk of skin cancer. UVA rays penetrate deeper into the skin, contributing to collagen and elastin breakdown, fine lines, and premature aging. They can even penetrate windows, making daily protection important when you’re indoors, too.

When it comes to sunscreen formulas, there are two main types of UV filters: mineral and chemical. Mineral sunscreens use zinc oxide, titanium dioxide, or both to protect the skin, primarily by absorbing UV radiation, with some reflection and scattering. Chemical sunscreens also absorb UV rays and convert that energy into less harmful forms, primarily heat. Both can provide effective sun protection, although their textures and how they feel on the skin can vary. Mineral formulas are often recommended for sensitive skin, while chemical formulas tend to be lighter and less likely to leave a white cast.

SPF Level: “Picking a sunscreen does not have to be complicated!” Los Angeles dermatologist Chris Tomassian says. “Look for a sunscreen that is SPF 30 or higher and broad-spectrum, meaning it blocks both UVA and UVB rays. You want to use at least two finger lengths of sunscreen for the full face, and when you are out in the sun, reapply every two hours to maintain adequate sun protection.” Apply sunscreen at least 15 minutes before going outside.

Type: Mineral sunscreens use zinc oxide, titanium dioxide, or both to protect against UV radiation, primarily through absorption, with some reflection and scattering. Chemical sunscreens also absorb UV rays, converting their energy into less harmful forms. “Although both can be incredibly effective, chemical sunscreens can be somewhat more irritating to sensitive skin,” explains Dr. Nazarian. “Physical sunscreens are a bit more stable, but are also a little more occlusive and can trap heat, so it may feel a little heavier than the chemical sunscreens.” Zinc oxide and titanium dioxide are mineral sunscreen ingredients to look for.

Texture: How the sunscreen sits on the skin can make all the difference in how it wears. Those looking for something under makeup might appreciate a lightweight formula or something with a primer-like texture. Conversely, someone with dry skin might consider a richer, emollient formula similar to a face cream. That being said, keep your routine (and skin type) in mind when selecting your SPF this year.

White Cast: A white cast is a whitish, almost purplish hue left behind after applying some sunscreens. This often happens with mineral formulas that utilize zinc oxide to provide a physical barrier between the skin and sun rays. Fortunately, most sunscreens these days—even mineral options—do not leave a white cast.

Broad-Spectrum: Broad-spectrum means your sunscreen protects against both UVA and UVB rays. If a sunscreen is not labeled “broad-spectrum,” it may not provide adequate UVA protection, leaving your skin more vulnerable to sun damage and premature aging.

Editors’ Review: Our editorial team has tested each sunscreen on this list (and more that weren’t featured!), so we can speak to everything from texture and finish to how well they layer under makeup. Where our editors feel particularly strongly about a formula, you’ll find their firsthand experiences alongside insights from our dermatologist experts.

“Broad-spectrum describes the quality of providing sun protection against a wide range of ultraviolet wavelengths,” Dr. Murphy-Rose explains. “Broad-spectrum sunscreens protect against both UVA and UVB rays.”

Dr. Murphy-Rose recommends applying two finger lengths of product to the face each application. “One of the most common mistakes in sunscreen application is not applying enough product,” she continues. “Always remember to apply to your whole face, including over the brows, ears, and around the lips. These are areas where I very often see pre-cancerous skin growths, and I believe that’s partly due to less sunscreen use on these areas.” She also recommends using an SPF lip balm.

There’s an ongoing debate about mineral versus chemical sunscreen. In fact, you might be wondering what those terms even mean, and which you should use in your routine. Here’s what you need to know:

What Sunscreen Is Recommended by Dermatologists?

In our reporting, dermatologists often recommended broad-spectrum sunscreens with SPF 30 or higher from EltaMD, SkinCeuticals, La Roche-Posay, and Supergoop!. For more insight into their picks, see the expert recommendations featured throughout our product reviews.

Should You Wear Sunscreen Every Day—Even Indoors?

Wearing sunscreen every day helps protect against cumulative sun damage, which can contribute to skin cancer and premature aging. And while you might think you’re safe from UV rays indoors, UVA radiation can penetrate ordinary window glass, making sunscreen particularly worthwhile if you spend time near sunny windows. Visible light from the sun can also contribute to hyperpigmentation, especially in people with darker skin tones or melasma. However, there’s little evidence that the blue light emitted by phones, computers, and other electronic devices has a meaningful effect on the skin.

How to Apply (and Reapply) Sunscreen Properly

Now that you know what to look for in a sunscreen, it’s important to apply it with care. “It is a common issue that consumers are not applying it correctly, whether applying a product that is substandard, applying an insufficient amount, or neglecting to reapply appropriately,” Dr. Murphy-Rose explains. “A good guideline for sufficient coverage is about a shot glass’s worth of product for one application for the whole body or the equivalent of two finger lengths for the face.”

If you’re in direct sunlight, you should reapply sunscreen at least every two hours, as well as after swimming, sweating, or towel drying. This means applying the same amount you did initially (read: the two-finger rule!). Sunscreen sprays and powders can be convenient for touch-ups, especially over makeup, but it can be difficult to apply enough to achieve the SPF protection stated on the label. For the most reliable coverage, stick with a lotion or cream when reapplying. According to Dr. Tomassian, another misconception is that makeup with SPF is sufficient for your daily sunscreen: your SPF-laced makeup must be layered on top of a layer of sunscreen. “Always remember the sunscreen that you will use is the one that is going to be most effective!” Dr. Karen adds.

Sunscreen should generally be reapplied at least every two hours when you’re outdoors, and more frequently after swimming, sweating, or towel drying, according to the product’s water-resistance instructions. Over time, sunscreen can wear off or become less effective, so regular reapplication is essential to maintain protection.

You might be wondering: Does SPF matter? It’s a common question we beauty editors get, especially with so many different sunscreens on the market. Not to mention, you might receive different advice based on who you’re talking to.

“SPF (Sun Protection Factor) measures the amount of UV radiation needed to produce sunburn on protected skin (with sunscreen) compared to the amount of UV radiation required to produce sunburn on unprotected skin (without sunscreen),” Dr. Green explains. “The higher the SPF, the more UV radiation exposure your skin can handle before getting sunburned.” Generally speaking, most experts recommend an SPF of 30 or higher, but Dr. Green advises a minimum of SPF 50.

When we test and review a product, we take a holistic approach to deliver well-rounded product recommendations. First, we lean on Vogue’s vast network of experts—from board-certified dermatologists to celebrity estheticians—for professional insight into the industry’s standout products that specialists use themselves or recommend to clients. We pair their expertise with our editorial best practices to curate the thoughtful edits you read on our site.

For sunscreens, we selected the best based on the following characteristics: ingredients, texture, finish, SPF level, and the skin-care concerns they address. To do this, we paired our own tests of each formula with expert guidance and reviewer insights to determine which we would recommend.

#6710. Skyler Gisondo Is Using His First Awards Show Circuit to Experiment With Style

Last night, ahead of the 2026 Governors Awards, Primetime actor Skyler Gisondo put the finishing touches on his Louis Vuitton look in a rather unusual location: the projection room at the Aero Theater in Santa Monica.

The theater hosted a Gisondo-themed double feature: Primetime and Licorice Pizza (you may also know him from his scene-stealing performance in Booksmart). Still, this is all new to him. Though Gisondo has been a working actor since childhood, Primetime marks his first-ever awards season campaign. “It’s not even a dream come true because it’s almost like something I never even allowed myself to dream of,” Gisondo tells Vogue.

With an awards blitz comes extra attention to fashion. “I’m 30, and I feel like I just recently started to figure out what I like,” says Gisondo, who loves a vintage workman jacket and admits, “I seem unable to eat anything without spilling on myself, so that is kind of prohibitive for me wanting to get nicer shirts and stuff.”

Enter menswear maestro Jason Bolden, who is helping Gisondo achieve a look inspired by his favorite actors: Redford and Newman. “I think there’s a certain timelessness to it—it’s cool in any era at any time. That’s certainly the stuff that I find myself responding to,” he says.

While he’s kicking off awards season with a classic black suit (though the Vuitton tuxedo blouson adds a cool, unfussy touch), Bolden is nudging the actor toward bolder pieces. “I’m pretty tame in my fashion decisions,” Gisondo says. “I’m probably Jason’s most boring client, but to me, the stuff he has me in, I’m like, oh my God, this is crazy.” What is crazy, you may ask? “He’ll put me in a leather jacket or something, and I’ll look at him and say, ‘Jason, I don't know if I’m this guy, man.’ And he’ll say, ‘Oh, you’re this guy.’”

Bolden isn’t the only one encouraging Gisondo to broaden his fashion horizons. “My girlfriend is also lovingly pushing me to dress cooler,” he says. He recalls an instance when she suggested he buy a pair of baggy pants. “I was like, ‘I just can’t do it. They’re too big. This just isn’t me.’ Now they’re not even the biggest pants I own.”

Up next on Bolden’s agenda? To get Gisondo into a pair of leather pants.

“If I learned anything from Primetime, it’s that you just got to try things. You can’t be afraid to fail,” he says. “You can’t be afraid to fail at work, in your fashion choices. You only live once. Let’s try some things. Put on the leather pants. Let’s see how it goes.”

Here, Skyler Gisondo brings Vogue to the projection room ahead of the 2026 Governors Awards.

In the room where the magic happens—the projection room.

#6707. All the Photos of Princess Kate and Prince William on Their Overseas Tour of Mumbai

The royal pair will spend the next three days in India for the 2026 Earthshot Prize Awards

Early this morning, Princess Kate and Prince William landed in Mumbai, India, for their first overseas royal tour in four years. The last time the pair made an international state visit was when they traveled to the Caribbean in the spring of 2022 to celebrate the late Queen Elizabeth II’s Platinum Jubilee.

Now, the Prince and Princess of Wales have traveled to the Indian city for the 2026 Earthshot Prize Awards, which are being hosted in Asia for the first time ever. The annual prize, founded by Prince William with an inaugural ceremony in 2021 and set to last until 2030, celebrates innovators who are breaking ground in the environmentalism space.

While it was announced back in February that William would be traveling for the ceremony, it was confirmed only this past weekend that Kate would be joining him. This marks her first major trip with William since her 2024 cancer diagnosis and eventual remission (she also took a solo trip to Italy this May).

The 2026 Earthshot ceremony is set to take place tomorrow night, but Kate and William are spending a total of three days in India. They began with a trip to the Ballard Estate, the business and shopping district of Mumbai. There, the Prince and Princess of Wales played a bit of gully cricket, took a ride on the metro, and visited local vendors and performers.

For the rest of their time abroad, the royals will be “celebrating innovation, sustainability, youth leadership, and the enduring connections between the United Kingdom and India,” per their office at Kensington Palace.

#6702. Loaded Mashed Potatoes

Mashed potatoes are a classic side dish that can make almost any meal better. They’re creamy, fluffy, and nearly perfect…nearly. When thinking of how to improve on a dish already so close to perfection, I decided to go back to the basics. What’s the best kind of potato? A loaded baked potato. With this in mind, I combined the two superstar spuds into one glorious dish. In it, light, airy mashed potatoes are topped with all of the classic baked potato fillings—cheddar cheese, chives, bacon, and sour cream—for a dish that truly embodies the best of both worlds.

The beauty of these potatoes is that they can go with any number of dinners. Serve them alongside your favorite protein—chicken, steak, pork chops, the world is your oyster—or alongside a few lighter sides like any of our green salads, caramelized Brussels sprouts, or roasted green beans.

Any leftovers can be stored in an airtight container in the fridge for 3 to 4 days. If you haven’t already added the toppings, store them separately. Whatever you do, don’t let leftovers go to waste. We’ve got plenty of leftover mashed potato recipes to make sure you get the most out of the special spuds.

#6701. Anthony Boyle Plays the Ultimate Boy-Wonder Tech Bro in ‘The Altruists’

Before Anthony Boyle and I begin our interview, we’ve already covered a fair amount of ground. Serendipitously, we’ve ridden up alongside each other on our bicycles. Boyle is still acquainting himself with the city’s two-wheeled possibilities; he’s a recent but enthusiastic convert. “It opens up the world!” he declares.

Our first stop is a tiny gallery in east London, where an exhibition of contemporary Irish art is on display. Boyle, a Belfast transplant, has lived in London off and on for the past few years. “Is that the Big Fish? It’s surely the Big Fish,” Boyle asks of one painting, invoking the giant salmon sculpture that sits on the waterfront in Belfast. The actor is wearing jeans, low-profile sneakers, and a blue T-shirt with a horse printed on the back, a recent impulse purchase—it reminds him of childhood summer vacations spent riding in Donegal.

Recently, Boyle, 32, has established himself with his shrewd portrayal of IRA commander Brendan “The Dark” Hughes in the TV series Say Nothing and his assured turn as the flamboyant Arthur Guinness in Steven Knight’s House of Guinness. Earlier this year, the film I See Buildings Fall Like Lightning—in which Boyle plays a college-educated food-delivery cyclist—premiered to impassioned reviews at Cannes. “The film feels like a socialist war cry,” Boyle says, describing its portrayal of working-class communities in modern Britain.

And this November, he took another exhilarating left turn, starring opposite Julia Garner in Netflix’s The Altruists, an eight-part series about the spectacular collapse of the cryptocurrency exchange FTX—and one of a handful of new films and TV series that spin our recent techno-history into entertainment. Boyle plays the company’s disgraced founder, Sam Bankman-Fried, whose dizzying ascent and precipitous downfall have made him a hubristic emblem of the crypto age. The show casts Bankman-Fried and his on-again, off-again girlfriend and colleague Caroline Ellison (Garner) as hyperintelligent idealists, at once fiercely competitive and committed to “effective altruism”—a philosophical movement that seeks to optimize charitable giving.

The awkward physicality, unruly hair, and peculiar vocal inflections that Boyle brought to the role are a world away from the steely conviction of an IRA soldier or the patrician swagger of a Guinness. For his first meeting with The Altruists’ creators, he went to the budget retailer Primark, bought a black sweater, smeared it with Cup Noodle stains, and mussed his hair. He logged onto Zoom in character, improvising that he had Mark Zuckerberg waiting on another call. His childhood friend and Say Nothing costar Lola Petticrew, with whom he rehearsed, offered invaluable feedback. “When you do that voice, I want to throw a cup of tea over you,” Boyle remembers Petticrew saying. He was delighted. “There’s an inner conflict in him that makes his performances unpredictable,” says Garner. “Whatever he’s thinking or feeling, you can see it in his eyes. You can’t teach someone to have those eyes.”

Once he was offered the role, Boyle watched footage of Bankman-Fried, fascinated by an awkwardness at odds with his extraordinary influence. He even corresponded with Bankman-Fried in prison: “This is the smartest person I’ve ever been in contact with,” he says of their exchange. “People don’t think they’re doing evil,” Boyle says, reflecting on Bankman-Fried’s motivations. “They believe they’re doing things for the greater good.”

PARALLEL LINES Anthony Boyle wears a Dries Van Noten sweater, JW Anderson jeans, and Paul Smith belt.

As a boy in West Belfast, Boyle trained at the Royal Welsh College of Music & Drama before his onstage breakout at age 21 as Scorpius Malfoy in Harry Potter and the Cursed Child, which earned him an Olivier Award. “Before that, my longest play was a five-day run at a community center,” he says. In addition to his striking Say Nothing and House of Guinness performances, Boyle’s career has encompassed roles in the World War II drama Masters of the Air and the John Wilkes Booth–inspired series Manhunt. “I met with a bunch of cowboys,” he says of that role, “it took me a while to lose the habit of picking up random things, chewing, spitting them out.” He delights in the contrasts in his work: “I’m always trying to do what I haven’t done,” he says. As we walk through the park, a few men practicing martial arts catch Boyle’s attention, and he acquires a pair of nunchucks, giving them a whirl with enough confidence that I can’t discern whether this is a genuine skill or a gloriously committed bluff. “Pretty good, right?” he asks.

Since The Altruists finished shooting last November, Boyle completed the forthcoming dark comedy The Worst (with Keira Knightley and Alicia Vikander) and has been filming Charmer, a new series in which he plays a professional seducer hired to test the fidelity of his wealthy clients’ partners. Next comes a second season of House of Guinness, with just two days off between filming. “I need to find little bits of joy and craic where I can,” he says.

One of those joys is returning home. Boyle has lived in London and New York and recently moved back to Belfast, although his schedule means he seldom spends much time there. Recently at a McDonald’s with his cousin, Boyle’s order was supersized without any prompting. “Anything for The Dark,” the server said—a reference to his Say Nothing role, and, Boyle reckons, just about the coolest thing that could have happened on his home turf. He’s been honored by his hometown in additional ways: Belfast artist Colin Davidson recently painted his portrait and unveiled it at the city’s Lyric Theatre. Davidson offered him some sage advice while the actor was sitting for him: “He said, ‘Never soften your edges, Anthony.’ ”

“Coming from where we come from, this career seemed a world away, but seeing him chase it made it feel more tangible for me,” says Petticrew. Working together on Say Nothing, Petticrew adds, was meaningful beyond the acting: “To reflect the place we both love so deeply and tell its story was probably one of the most important things we’ll ever do.”

An upcoming project will bring Boyle even nearer to his origins. In a four-part adaptation of Michael Magee’s acclaimed 2023 novel Close to Home, he plays Sean, a young man returning to a Belfast reckoning with the aftermath of the sectarian violence of the Troubles. Boyle attended the same school as Magee a few years apart, and the novel depicts a world Boyle recognizes—down to the hardscrabble streets and poorly paid nightclub jobs. “I felt like someone was reaching out of the book and grabbing me,” he says. “It articulated thoughts and experiences I thought only I had.” Boyle urged the book on everyone around him. “I bought a hundred copies,” he says—one Christmas, he distributed 10 among his family. “None of them opened it.” Television, he realized, might be a way to get the story into their lives.

Playing Sean demanded a new methodology from Boyle: “It was almost coming back to myself in a way and not performing,” he says. Boyle, who is also executive producing the series, championed local talent and developed it closely with Magee, running scenes with him until the dialogue felt lived-in. “I wanted to make Close to Home for people in the areas I grew up in.”

We’re wrapping up our walk when he tells me he is meeting his father to help his younger sister move apartments. She recently arrived in London for college and was temporarily living in his house with a friend while looking for a place of her own. Boyle would leave for work before dawn and return late to find them blasting Sinéad O’Connor and dancing in the kitchen. “It was infectious,” he says. We’re now in front of Hackney City Farm, a cloistered urban oasis with livestock and gardens. His new T-shirt has reminded him how much he misses riding, and he wants to know where in London he can get on a horse. When I mention that the farm has donkeys, he’s thrilled: “I love a donkey.”

A week later, I run into Boyle again, shouting to me as I bike past. This time he’s accompanied by his older sibling and their partner. He’s showing them around before heading to New York for The Altruists press tour. Swaggering along the road, he is just as buoyant as before, taking himself off on another adventurous detour.

In this story: Grooming, Liz Taw; tailor, Nafisa Tosh.

#6700. Joe Budden Can't Stop Betting on Himself

This is an edition of the weekly newsletter Tap In, GQ senior associate editor Frazier Tharpe’s final word on the most heated online discourse about music, movies, and TV. Sign up here to get it free.

There’s Joe Budden, and then there’s everyone else. Despite the proliferation of rapper-led podcasts and talk shows in the last few years (itself a subset of the ever-ballooning celebrity podcast bubble), there’s no question who leads the pack—at least that’s how Budden sees it. It’s hard to argue with him: in February, Budden’s self-titled and pioneering podcast will turn 12,. Not too long ago, he posted a screenshot that “accidentally” revealed his yearly take-home was a number in the low eight figures.

Through it all, there've been dramatic cast changes, tense moments with rappers, hilarious must-see breakdowns of big moments in culture, and several dust-ups with Drake, a frequent subject of Budden and his co-host’s. Being part-New Jerseyian, I’m one of the vocal minority that believe it’s a travesty to reduce Budden’s rap career to “Pump It Up” (this is never far from my Recently Played to this day). Our paths actually crossed during his time at Complex, when I served as a sit-in for test episodes of Everyday Struggle while he searched for the co-host that would eventually become Akademiks—and even helped them launch the show with a series of digital short skits I wrote and produced that are probably lost to the internet by now.

As an occasional pod listener, I find it fascinating that Budden has defied all odds, and everyone who said he was bound to burn bridges, and now finds himself commanding attention, revenue and even reconciliations with his past employers. Despite how things went with State of the Culture, he’s back in business with Revolt. Joe Budden Network Presents, his new weekly series, premieres on November 13th, bringing his podcast to linear television through a unique micro-licensing partnership that allows Joe to retain full ownership of his IP.

To mark his next chapter, we talked about the current landscape of rap media and podcasting, the dangers of getting too comfortable, and how he plans to maintain his reign.

#6699. Rashida Jones and Will McCormack on Telling Sex Jokes and Building The Invite Around Its “Prodigiously Gifted” Cast

The two screenwriters adapted Cesc Gay’s The People Upstairs (2020) for a modern American audience

This summer, The Invite dropped audiences into a turbulent middle-aged marriage where only bitterness simmers beneath a thin, leftover layer of love and any sexual desire vanished long ago. The couple at the center of the story, Joe (Seth Rogen) and Angela (Olivia Wilde, who also directed the film), live at each other’s throats, feigning a relationship for their daughter’s sake. This brittle romance is put to the test when a neighboring couple (Ed Norton and Penélope Cruz) come over for dinner and propose, well, group sex. Bottled-up emotions and memories fill the night as four dinner guests banter and battle, delivering the best sex comedy in years, elevated by witty, cathartic dialogue from screenwriters Rashida Jones and Will McCormack.

A great sex comedy relies on a great premise, whether it’s designed to shock or move its audience. Jones and McCormack reworked Spanish filmmaker Cesc Gay’s The People Upstairs (also known as Sentimental), crafting a script that balances both, exploring how romantic turmoil emerges from a culture of emotional suppression and what happens when this lifestyle rubs up against a more radically open approach to feelings and sex. Much of the realism in The Invite comes from Jones and McCormack closely studying their own relationships with love and loss.

“We had all these big dreams when we were young,” Jones says. “It’s a real embarrassment of riches. It’s when you get to 50, and you’ve had a lot of your dreams come true, and you have all the things that you dreamed about when you were 20, 30, and even 40. Here we are, and what now? The ‘what now’ is infused in the movie.”

Decades of friendship have informed Jones and McCormack’s writing. They first met on a blind date in 1999, but the romantic relationship did not stick; in its place, a creative partnership blossomed. In 2012, they released their first full-length feature, Celeste and Jesse Forever, a bittersweet examination of divorce and friendship starring Jones and Andy Samberg.

“When we were young, we were writing about the heartbreak of love, and now we’re writing about the heartbreak of life,” McCormack says. “Marriage is not this triumphant experience. It’s a broken hallelujah; it’s one day at a time. That’s the beauty of it: the endurance of it and the longevity of it.”

Bazaar sat down and spoke with the pair about tailoring their script around a quartet of legendary actors, the “accordion effect” of Olivia Wilde’s direction, and how the two screenwriters have bounced ideas (and sex jokes) off each other for three decades.

Rashida Jones: In 2022, we were approached by David Permit, one of our wonderful producers, and we were hired to write a script for a whole different version of this movie for different directors. It was probably the most fun I’ve ever had, writing that first draft with Will. We just had a great time because we saw the movie and it totally works and it’s culturally specific. We watched it at first, and we thought, “Wow, it’s perfectly executed. What can we contribute?” Then you watch it again and again and again, and you start having conversations about your own life and your own relationships and people that are on your mind. We started to feel like we might have something to say in this world and that could add value and be more culturally specific to being American. How we would have this conversation—this evening, in our country. It felt like the perfect combination of source material and then a place to grow.

RJ: I’ll take the easy part. We’re just immature, and we like to make sex jokes to each other because we’ve been best friends for 30 years, so that was easy.

Will McCormack: There’s no DP. We brought all the DP.

WM: We were having fun with that stuff. Cesc Gay’s movie is so brilliant. When we watched it, you think, “Oh my God, this is so beautiful.” But then, as Rashida said, you start to think about it more, and you say, “Wouldn’t it be fun to do this?” And then you get the job, and you actually get to do that. It’s so rare. When we watched the original one, I remember halfway through, we said to each other, he’s going to go in the other room at the end and play the piano, and it’s going to wreck us. And he didn’t. I liked their ending, but we thought, this is going to be so fun to dive into: [Seth Rogen’s character] pulling up the piano lid, sitting at the keys, and there’s this Graduate-style ending where she’s going to walk in and the camera is going to end on her face, and [we’re left thinking], is she in or is she out? That was our entry point to this version. It’s how we got the job, because we opened the conversation with the ending.

RJ: Rhythm is so important to us. The truth is, when you infuse a role with an actor, rhythms change because there are things that make sense coming out of people’s mouths and things that don’t. For us, this period before shooting was our rewrite period, which was insane because we were doing eight-hour days with the actors and getting a download of their ideas and concerns, then rewriting simultaneously. That period was really when the movie was the version of the movie on the screen. It was designed for these particular actors on the screen. Their cadences entered our heads, so we needed to rewrite with them in mind. We were trying our best Penélope Cruz and Seth Rogen impressions.

WC: These actors are all prodigiously gifted and giants. They all brought their own pathos and idiosyncrasies. Penélope brought this incredible mystery and mystique and aura. Edward brought this radical honesty. Olivia brought a woman on the verge of a nervous breakdown at all times. Seth brought this profound simplicity. So it was such a gift to work with them and help tailor these parts to their voices.

RJ: Penélope had a really strong instinct. She’s one of our best. What can I possibly say about her? She really wanted to talk about perimenopause, which I too have that cross to bear at the moment. She wanted to get into it and was very conscious that her character has depth. She didn’t want her to have this wise, objective point of view. She wanted to have her own life and flaws. All that stuff comes out at the end, where she bites back at Hawk and tells him to shut the fuck up and then apologizes quickly. You get a window into their relationship. That really came out of conversations of “How can we show how imperfect their very new relationship is? How can we show that everybody’s struggling with their version of an issue?” It’s not all perfect over there just because they have figured out a way to live in an open relationship.

WC: The magic of movie-making is that there are a million different versions of The Invite. This version could’ve only happened at this moment in time, in this city, with this director, with these four people. It’s magic. I was most surprised by Penélope, who was so smart and didn’t want to feel like this wise woman on the hilltop delivering sage advice to these people in the room. Because life is not like that. It’s messy and complicated, and she has her own problems. When actors are that good, they just surprise you over and over again. We’ve said it before, but the best element of storytelling and screenwriting is surprise—when you don’t know what’s going to happen next and you’re on the edge of your seat.

WC: Edward is one of the best actors we have as well. He is so smart. Right from the very beginning of the rehearsal process, he wanted. He was adamant that we talk a lot about Hawk’s backstory and that his sexual expansiveness came from a place of real pain and trauma. We talked a lot about Hawk’s backstory. We talked about how he had massive regret and pain and that he had a moment in his life where he was going to fold or turn over a new leaf and live without regret. It was incredible to talk to him about it. We never heard the final version [of the speech until the day we filmed].

RJ: He wanted to surprise the actors. We wrote him a template, a blueprint, so he had that. Then he could do whatever he wanted because he had something in mind. The sentiment was there, and it was something we had talked about because we wanted to make sure whatever, wherever it ended up, had to be tender and raw. Coming out of that, it gave Joe another opportunity to be uncomfortable with feelings. He could just make a joke, and that would set off the next part of the interaction, so everybody could be mad at him for being immature.

WC: We have Joe’s line slotted there, “I’m sorry that your dead wife couldn’t peg you,” which then detonates the entire thing again. Olivia did such a great job of orchestrating this accordion effect of quiet to blow up to quiet.

RJ: We have our go-tos. Nora Ephron. We’re always watching James L. Brooks for rhythm, pacing, and dialogue. Of course, Who’s Afraid of Virginia Woolf? was a reference for us because it’s a movie we grew up both loving and a play we love. It has this chamber-piece tension that you need to keep. There are literal steals and direct references to that movie in our version, [such as including a character who works as] an associate professor. Movies like Bob & Carol & Ted & Alice and, in general, Paul Mazursky for the reality of being in a messy relationship.

WC: [Ruben Östlund’s] Force Majeure and always Joachim Trier for the honesty of people being really vulnerable with each other. It’s never corny, and it’s really heartbreaking and true and admirable. The end of The Invite gets very real when she says, “We were best friends, and we had sex all the time, and it was incredible.” There is a moment where it really lands.

RJ: Everybody’s so different. We talk a lot because, for us, theme is king. We want to make sure we know what we want to say. We identify how we want to feel and how we want other people to feel, and we write toward that. We’re not going to leave feeling like there’s a bad guy; we want to leave feeling like we root for them as people, separately and together. Whatever happens after this night, this was a necessary step in excavating the deepest emotions and truth that were never revealed, and they are left vulnerable with tenderness and gratitude for the relationship they did have. We talk in those terms, and then we get into structure, and then we can talk about which scenes are which and how we get there.

WC: Luckily, we’ve known each other for so long, and we’re so comfortable with each other. That’s so important to a partnership. I’m not afraid to pitch any idea to Rashida and vice versa. I’m not afraid to write poorly. I feel total freedom of expression, which is an important thing in collaboration. Our lives have orbited each other in an interesting way as friends, but also creatively. We’re both really into loss, and life is a series of losses. There’s so much heartbreak and loss, but there’s so much hope and humor. The hardest moments of our lives have been the funniest, and it’s been a gift to write it all down with her. Hopefully, we can do it again!

#6698. Discover the Best Dresses for Every November Occasion

All products featured on Vogue are independently selected by our editors. However, we may earn affiliate revenue on this article and commission when you buy something.

No matter the occasion or season, there are always plenty of opportunities to wear a dress and, of course, an overwhelming number of options from which to pick. We’ve put together an edit of the best dresses of the month; check back every two weeks for a new selection of styles, handpicked by Vogue.

There’s no better time than mid-November to embrace a little holiday cheer. Based on this month’s standout dress finds, the key elements are texture, color, and fabric. Art Deco influences were plenty on the fall runways, and drop-waist styles like Tory Burch’s deep red midi make for excellent party attire. ‘Tis also the season for autumnal textures in wool, velvet, jacquard—try a brocade number for an evening occasion, or pick up a sweater dress from either Me+Em or Proenza Schouler to wear throughout the winter. And for all fall weddings and holiday festivities on the horizon, there are simply too many good options—from Toteme to Staud—that will make you the best-dressed guest.

Whether you’re shopping for new essentials, vacation, or a destination wedding, these are November’s best dresses.

Cos reimagines the LBD with swishy godet panels, for a flattering, effortless silhouette. Pair it with flats for the office or kitten heels after dark. Did I mention this makes for a perfect cocktail dress?

The classic slip dress gets a romantic update in satin and Chantilly lace. An elegant choice for holiday soirées or the bride-to-be.

Toteme puts a fresh spin on the reverse collar trend with this pale pink silk dress. The draped silhouette makes for effortless office dressing.

A gala, a wedding, or an evening at the ballet? With its decadent embellishments and Art Deco allure, this dress is the epitome of 1920s glamour, fit for right now.

A favorite from Kallmeyer’s holiday capsule, this beaded mini is made for after parties. Style it with sheer tights and strappy sandals.

Fresh off Tory Burch’s fall 2026 runway, this drop-waist dress is ready for the holiday circuit. Pair with embellished heels for extra festive flair.

Two looks in one. Me+Em’s versatile set pairs a sleeveless turtleneck dress with a coordinating long-sleeve sweater. Drape the merino wool knit over your shoulders or tie it around your waist.

As temperatures dip, this textured bouclé dress offers a chic alternative to traditional winter knits. Finish the look with a ’90s-inspired tailored coat for understated elegance.

RSVP, yes! This dazzling purple dress makes a compelling case for bold color at upcoming weddings.

Decadent velvet without the decadent price tag? Zara delivers with this sumptuous crushed velvet gown. A true steal!

Taller Marmo’s red mini is a statement in itself. Pair it with sheer tights, strappy sandals, and a pouch for holiday events, or go bare-legged for a glamorous beach event.

Cooler temperatures are no reason to retire color. Consider this your invitation to embrace it. Fforme’s elegant take on the evening dress features a dropped waist, mock neckline, and sleeveless silhouette—all in a rich berry hue.

What’s a fall wardrobe without a knit dress? This under-$300 find from Banana Republic makes for a go-to in any wardrobe. Style it with knee-high boots for effortless cold-weather dressing.

Tory Burch sent this printed dress—in a trompe l’oeil pattern resembling tiger stripes—down the runway in two iterations: a delicate pastel jacquard and a rich purple version. With a fitted bodice that cascades into a handkerchief hemline, it’s a romantic silhouette that needs little extra to feel styled. A close-cut jacket and patent pumps should do the trick.

A knit dress is always a good idea. Simkhai’s ribbed iteration, accented with a delicate white trim, makes a subtle yet striking statement. Add a knee-high boot, a sweeping opera coat, and a chic clutch.

A dress that’ll get you multiple double-takes. This red maxi with a built-in cummerbund reimagines traditional black tie dress codes for a feminine-meets-masculine twist.

ICYMI: Jacquard is having a moment. This black V-neck dress, accented with a cummerbund sash, brings a polished edge to evening dressing. Even better? It’s under $300.

Conner Ives can do no wrong. Spaghetti straps, delicate pleats, and a vibrant color story—the dress is composed of four different printed vintage scarves—make this a vacation must-have, whether you’re spending Thanksgiving in Nayarita or St. Barths.

The shift is a forever classic and Posse’s silk-satin take is minimalist in the best way. Bring that less is more ethos to accessorizing with a simple pouch and drop earrings.

Emily Dawn Long—a CFDA 2026 runner-up—has a knack for intuiting what we want to wear before we do. This bright color-blocked midi is just one recent example.

Consider this sleeveless, funnel-neck knit dress a blank canvas for styling. Layer it over or under a long-sleeve knit (for extra warmth), throw on a sweeping shearling coat, then finish with flat boots. The possibilities are endless for cold-weather dressing.

You know the LBD, you (we hope) love the LBD. Play with proportions to give it a fresh look: a long duster coat, statement brooch, and polished loafers toughen up its feminine spirit.

Why yes, it’s Kallmeyer’s fall collection opening look: a creamy drop-waist style made from a silk charmeuse with caped armholes and a high funnel-neck. Follow the fall show’s styling notes and pair it with patent leather knee-highs–a pulled-back bun and minimal jewelry give the dress its well-deserved moment.

Maria McManus first introduced this dress in an off-white shade; this rich eggplant hue makes for a wonderful autumnal option.

Khaite’s chiffon maxi strikes the perfect balance between delicate and wearable. Give it heft by styling with a leather coat, tights, and strappy sandals.

Weddings or galas on the horizon? This strapless satin gown—as seen on supermodel Karlie Kloss, who wore it earlier this year for Taylor Swift’s wedding—works for either occasion.

Minimal, elegant, and endlessly versatile, this long-sleeve satin number is the kind of wardrobe staple that moves easily from desk job to after-hours plans. Dress it up with pumps or keep things understated with a flat—it looks sophisticated however your style it.

Kallmeyer’s Ivette dress is easily one of the season’s most talked-about dresses. With Art Deco influences leading the trend conversation, its embellished design feels especially on-trend.

Feathers, feathers, and more feathers! Whether you’re dressing for a special occasion or are a bride-to-be, this Loulou de Saison dress is designed to make an entrance. The drop-waist silhouette, adorned with feather embellishments around the hips, delivers just the right amount of drama. More is more!

Print enthusiasts, look no further than this floral maxidress from Banana Republic. Rendered in a rich persimmon print, the silhouette skims the body before flaring into a sweeping skirt. A romantic take on fall florals.

J.Crew’s cult-favorite rollneck sweater has officially entered its dress era. Just in time for sweater weather, its simple silhouette makes a strong case for becoming your new go-to. Style it with hosiery and loafers, then tie a colorful knit around your for an extra layer.

ICYMI: Purple is the color to own this season, and this violet maxi from Zara features an alluring open-back silhouette. Not quite ready to bare it all? Layer a lightweight top underneath, either in a neutral hue or a contrasting pop of color.

Caftans can be worn year-round—you just have to pick one in a more fall-friendly fabric. Taller Marmo’s long-sleeve is cut from double satin, with adjustable buttons that allows you to wear it in more ways than one—off-the-shoulder, single-shoulder, or or with a boat neckline.

Velvet chartreuse has never looked better, courtesy of Reformation x Alexa Chung, whose recent collaboration delivered one of the finest evening dresses of the month. Pair it with a satin bag and kitten heels.

This Staud dress creates the illusion of separates: A fitted black silhouette contrasts with a white draped skirt. A study that proves that opposites really do attract.

Meryll Rogge’s debut collection for Marni has officially dropped. The color-block mini is cut from one of the collection’s most memorable prints: a playful floral print that captures the house’s signature eccentricity. Pair with loafers and a long necklace.

For a quiet statement, consider this tea-length dress from Merlette, crafted from a rich butter yellow satin with ruched sleeves. On chillier days, add a faux-fur coat on top.

Altuzarra’s flutter-sleeve dress balances romance and structure, with delicate draping that creates soft movement. Save this for a special occasion.

Straight from the runway, this sleeveless knit turtleneck is a master class in sculptural dressing, crafted from a stretch rib that contours the body and falls to a handkerchief hem.

Who needs a necklace when the dress is the jewelry? Brandon Maxwell turns a V-neck dress into a work of art, with a beaded neckline that moves like fringe. An elegant, embellished option for an evening affair.

No dress roundup is complete without a little black dress. This off-the-shoulder option from Fforme is cut from a thick scuba fabric for a sculpted look. Complete the idea with strappy sandals and an embellished evening bag.

This Tory Burch runway dress was an editor favorite. Cut from jacquard in a slate blue and cherry-check motif, the color combination creates an optical illusion of a rich purple hue (one of this season’s trending hues). Two versatile details make it especially wearable: Both the necktie and belt are removable. Complete the look with fall boots.

To celebrate Vogue’s book club selection Sense and Sensibility, Vogue collaborated on a limited-edition nightgown inspired by Jane Austen's heroines. A nightgown that isn't exclusive to home can be styled with a trench and equestrian-inspired boots for an effortlessly romantic look.

Simon Miller's take on the slip dress is both elegant and flattering. This dress works season after season; it's the kind of piece that always makes an entrance, whether paired with boots or sleek mules.

CJR is known for its bold use of color, and this dress is no exception. Inspired by the work of Ettore Sottsass, the graphic polo maxi makes for a statement for any occasion. Dress it up with pumps or style it with flat boots for the weekend.

For upcoming weddings or formal events, look to this artful Tove dress. Playful yet sophisticated, the halter-neck silhouette is elevated by sculptural detailing. Finish off with strappy sandals for a polished finish.

For a minidress to wear this fall, opt for Khaite's Scarla dress. The modern skater silhouette, cut from delicate silk chiffon, feels romantic yet contemporary, making it an especially chic option for a bride-to-be.

Call it the ultimate party dress. The holiday season may still be a little off, but it’s never too early to plan your party wardrobe. This embellished Posse mini will make you the best-dressed guest.

Zara has collaborated with filmmaker Luca Guadagnino and production designer Stefano Baisi on a collection spanning from ready-to-wear to homeware. This scarf-draped dress strikes the perfect balance of elegance and ease.

Minimal yet statement-making, this Magda Butrym gray dress has an off-the-shoulder neckline and a fitted silhouette. A tonal floral appliqué adds a feminine, sculptural finish. Pair it with kitten heels and a clutch for an elegant evening look.

When your wardrobe is craving a dose of color, look to yellow. This H&M Studio dress is cut from a lightweight fabric with romantic ruffle detailing, making it an effortless choice for a warm-weather holiday or a destination wedding.

For a fall dress, look to this minidress inspired by the ’60s. Cut from a wool blend, this double-breasted dress is an ideal layering piece. Style it with a colorful turtleneck or button-down and add tights and boots for a milder fall day, or pair it with loafers.

Alaïa's approach to construction is like no other, creating some of the most flattering silhouettes around. Cut from velvet, this tank dress makes for an understated yet impactful evening option—a minimalist statement.

Maygel Coronel’s dress proves that simplicity can still make an impact. The elongated drop waist, fluid draping, and bubble hem give the silhouette a fresh modern feel.

A fall wardrobe essential, this long-sleeve satin dress is a versatile piece that works from nine to nine. Polished enough for the office and elegant enough for an evening, it transitions seamlessly from day to night.

A black dress is a timeless choice, and this Altuzarra option interprets the style for modern wardrobes with draping and an asymmetric knotted waist. A chic choice for fall weddings: Just add a kitten heel and a clutch.

A little lace, or in this case a lot, goes a long way. This runway favorite from Khaite strikes the perfect balance between romantic and edgy. Ground it with sleek black shoes.

Violet! Purple! Amethyst! However you choose to call them, deep shades of purple are having a serious moment. Style with something red—a clutch or heels, or even a bold lip—for contrast.

Toteme makes the kind of dresses you’ll want to wear on repeat—and this draped maxi is one of them. New in for the season (we’ve written about it in cream, red, and butter yellow), this rich olive hue feels just right for fall.

Casual meets sophisticated in this fresh-off-the-runway Prada mini, featuring a drawstring waist and cultural motifs at the hem. Wear it over pants for an unexpected look.

Cut from a thick satin, Róhe’s sculptural one-shoulder dress contours the body just so, and needs little in the way of styling to feel complete; simply finish with a tassel clutch and kitten heels. Also available as a top, it’s equally chic styled as a full ensemble for an important event.

Color is all everyone can talk about this season—and chartreuse is one of the It hues. This Taller Marmo dress with an integrated cape makes for a striking wedding guest option.

A knit dress streamlines getting ready on a cold, rainy day, and this colorful knit intarsia style by Celine–as seen in the Vogue Shopping print guide to fall–is one worth investing in. Wear it with loafers on a crisp fall day, then switch to knee-high boots for the winter.

The kind of dress Carolyn Bessette-Kennedy might have worn. This simple boatneck style from Zara is minimal yet endlessly versatile.

Tory Burch knows her way around a shirtdress, and this style–straight off the fall runway–comes with a removable necktie, allowing you to switch up the look depending on the occasion.

From weddings to galas, look to this Fforme drop waist dress in blue sapphire hue. Pair with strappy sandals and a sleek pouch bag.

&Other Stories’s pleated mock neck dress is an effortless style that can easily be transformed for any occasion. Pair it with blazers and loafers for the office or dress it up with pumps for evening events.

For a lace dress that’s feminine but not overly sweet, look no further than Maria McManus’s black satin midi. Style it with a button-down or cardigan knotted at the waist, then add an unexpected pop of color with either your shoes or your bag.

A rich blue hue and draped silhouette give this dress a modern take on the 1920s drop-waist silhouette. Dress it up with heels for evening or ground it with loafers for an uptown-meets-downtown approach.

The nightie was a summer favorite, and for fall, Dôen gives it a polished update in a maxi silhouette with silk and lace trimmings. The result is a romantic yet refined style that works just as well at the office as it does for cocktails.

The LBD for minimalists comes courtesy of Toteme, who’ve crafted a short sleeve style from a bonded scuba crepe with darted seams for a subtly sculptural silhouette. Pair with tights and patent heels.

Khaite’s new fall collection has arrived, and this layered maxi, crafted from a sheer silk organza, is a standout. An elegant bustle detail at the back adds unexpected drama.

You’re not imaging it: Purple is one of the season’s standout shades and Toteme’s scarf dress is one of our favorite renditions. Finish with contrasting red or lemon hues for a jaunty color story.

Florals aren’t just reserved for spring. Ulla Johnson’s long-sleeve turtleneck dress makes the print feel autumnal. Pair with boots for this transitional period.

Kallmeyer’s Nala dress has Vogue bride written all over it. Keep the finishing touches pared-back with drop earrings and sleek white shoes.

Straight from Proenza Schouler’s fall runway is this artfully printed dress that’s great for any number of September occasions on your calendar. Pair it with kitten heels and a leather jacket for dinner out with friends, or with boots on cooler days.

Alaïa knows how to celebrate the female form, and this maxi from Pieter Mulier’s final collection for the house does just that. Crafted from ultra-soft merino, it’s also available in a zesty chartreuse, and classic black.

Not your typical minimalist ’90s slip. This olive-green version gets a romantic update with intricate lace trimming.

Whether you’re heading somewhere warm this month or simply leaning into fall’s version of artful bohemian dressing, a caftan always delivers. Easy and elegant, all you need is a long pendant and some chunky bangles to style it out.

COS reimagines the gather dress with a twisted detail at the front for asymmetric like draping. A no-brainer 9-5 option that needs minimal styling.
